{layout:"default",data:[{featuredPost:[{id:Z,title:_,slug:$,feature_image:aa,featured:d,published_at:ab,custom_excerpt:C,html:ac,authors:[{id:ad,name:ae,slug:af,profile_image:ag,cover_image:a,bio:ah,website:a,location:a,facebook:a,twitter:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a},{id:ai,name:aj,slug:ak,profile_image:al,cover_image:a,bio:am,website:a,location:a,facebook:a,twitter:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a}],tags:[{id:k,name:i,slug:j,description:l,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a},{id:e,name:f,slug:c,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a}],excerpt:C,reading_time:an,url:ao}],topPost:[{id:ap,title:aq,slug:ar,feature_image:as,featured:d,published_at:at,custom_excerpt:D,plaintext:"Cybercriminals are scaling fake online stores into a coordinated multinational business.\n\nA Bitdefender Labs investigation identified more than 55 fake-shop campaigns targeting consumers across 12 European countries between March and May 2026. The campaigns mimicked some of the world’s most recognizable brands, including Samsung, Nike, Adidas, ZARA, H&M, Amazon, Lidl, and SHEIN.\n\nAttackers used Facebook ads, WhatsApp messages, email campaigns, SMS messages, phone calls and fraudulent websites to lure victims into making payments, sharing personal information or buying fake goods.\n\nBitdefender researchers mapped more than 40 domains associated with these operations and observed repeat tactics that linked campaigns across multiple countries. Some operators relied on rotating domains and misleading redirects. Others built counterfeit supply chains through WhatsApp and password-protected catalogs. Several campaigns exploited anticipation surrounding the 2026 FIFA World Cup.\n\nThe findings show that fake-shop operations have evolved far beyond isolated scam websites. Many now resemble professional e-commerce businesses with advertising budgets, localization strategies and infrastructure designed to evade detection.\n\n\nKey takeaways\n\n * Researchers identified more than 55 confirmed fake-shop campaigns targeting consumers across Europe.\n * Attackers impersonated Samsung, Nike, Adidas, ZARA, H&M, Amazon, Lidl, SHEIN and other major brands.\n * Social media ads and WhatsApp emerged as the most-abused channels.\n * Several campaigns exploited FIFA World Cup 2026 excitement.\n * Operators reused infrastructure across countries and brands.\n * Redirect chains, Unicode lookalike domains, domain rotation, and localized content helped attackers evade detection.\n\n\n\nBy the numbers\n\n\n\n * 55+ confirmed fake-shop campaigns.\n * 15+ impersonated brands.\n * 12 European countries targeted.\n * 40+ fake domains mapped.\n * 6 attack channels observed.\n\n\n\nThe investigation tracked coordinated campaigns operating between March and May 2026 across social media platforms, WhatsApp, email, SMS, phone calls, and fraudulent e-commerce websites.\n\n\nFake-shop campaigns spread across Europe\n\nBitdefender security researchers have observed confirmed activity across the EU and the United Kingdom, including Germany, France, Italy, Poland, Spain, the Netherlands, Sweden, Portugal, Austria, Ireland and Romania.\n\nThe campaigns appeared on multiple channels:\n\n * Social media advertisements\n * WhatsApp messages\n * Email campaigns\n * SMS campaigns\n * Phone calls\n * Dedicated fake e-commerce websites\n\n\nRather than relying on a single fraudulent storefront, many operators rotated domains, changed brands, and adapted messaging to local audiences.\n\n\nSocial media ads impersonate trusted brands\n\n\nSamsung Galaxy S26 Ultra offered at 90% discounts\n\nOne campaign promoted Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra devices for only €249 while advertising discounts of up to 90%.\n\nThe operation targeted German consumers through Facebook advertisements and used domains such as:\n\n * shopintertec[.]com\n * shop.zppzddw[.]shop\n * shop.fjjfxxz[.]shop\n\n\nResearchers found a mix of unrelated advertising personas promoting the same fraudulent offer. The campaign generated substantial engagement, including hundreds of thousands of ad views.\n\nResearchers also saw a Netherlands-based variant that abused KPN branding. According to the investigation, ads promised Samsung-related offers through what appeared to be KPN-affiliated promotions, then redirected users to the fraudulent storefront crowndistrictstore[.]com.\n\n\nZARA and Nike campaigns shared the same infrastructure\n\nResearchers also identified a Polish hub operating through notcia[.]shop that impersonated both ZARA and Nike.\n\nThe ZARA campaign advertised Italian fashion products and seasonal collections while directing shoppers to fake storefronts at the same time. The Nike campaign promoted apparel and requested detailed delivery information, including home addresses and postal codes.\n\nResearchers also linked related domains to the operation, including:\n\n * nebularaless[.]info\n * shryxla-mynx[.]icu\n * isblkx[.]shop\n\n\nBoth campaigns used the same domain, notcia.shop, and the same advertising identity, suggesting an operational link between the ZARA and Nike impersonation activities. The Nike campaign also requested detailed delivery information, including house numbers and postal codes.\n\nMoreover, these campaigns seek to gather personal information from their targets. The website states the following (translated from Polish):\n\n\n\n\n“Please provide your exact delivery address (including street address, house number, and postal code), otherwise we cannot guarantee product delivery! Thank you for your cooperation! Processing time: Ships within 48-72 hours of payment. Money-back guarantee.”\n\n\n\n\nFake H&M sales targeted Italian shoppers\n\nAnother campaign promoted heavily discounted clothing through cyberloria[.]cfd.\n\nThis operation primarily targeted consumers in Italy and used Facebook advertisements that imitated legitimate H&M promotions. Attackers exploited brand recognition and discount claims to drive traffic toward a suspicious domain unrelated to the actual retailer.\n\n\nWhatsApp becomes a counterfeit marketplace\n\n\nThe “Supplier Carl” counterfeit network\n\nBitdefender researchers also uncovered a China-based operator calling himself “Carl” who contacted European users through WhatsApp. The messages promoted “1:1 quality” counterfeit products, including Nike, Adidas, Jordan, Golden Goose, various watches, luxury accessories and much more.\n\nVictims were directed toward password-protected Yupoo catalogs, including:\n\n * carl-album.x.yupoo[.]com\n * adidas555.x.yupoo[.]com\n * golden-goose.x.yupoo[.]com\n * xy666999.x.yupoo[.]com\n\n\nThe messages indicated that the operator offers DHL shipping to Europe and researchers observed the same infrastructure being promoted to users in Germany, France, and Italy.\n\nHere is an example of such a message (original text):\n\n\n\n\n“Nice to meet you, my name is Carl, I am a supplier from China. I specialize in 1:1 quality, I have shoes, clothes, bags, belts, watches, sunglasses and other products. About shipping. The express delivery for European countries is DHL, the shipping time is about 7-12 days. The express delivery for other countries is FedEx and the shipping time is 7 days. Here are some of my yupoo products. Please click to view. If you like the item, please send me a picture and I will tell you the price.”\n\n\n\n\nAdidas World Cup Fan-Kit scams\n\nAnother campaign targeted German users with messages promising free Adidas Deutschland 2026 Fan Kits.\n\nThe operation distributed thousands of WhatsApp messages and relied on redirect infrastructure such as:\n\n * linkrdr[.]cc\n * rewardpillar[.]cc\n * dealgo[.]cc\n * offerpilot[.]cc\n * msgdeal[.]cc\n\n\nThe campaign exploited enthusiasm for the FIFA World Cup to convince recipients that they had won exclusive promotional merchandise.\n\n\nUnicode lookalike domains bypass traditional checks\n\nResearchers also identified campaigns that abused visually deceptive domains—a surprisingly common tactic. Not everyone checks the actual URLs of the websites they open, and attackers know this all too well.\n\nExamples included:\n\n * adidȧs[.]com\n * ṇike[.]com\n * niḳe[.]com\n * rołex[.]com\n\n\nThese domains appeared legitimate to human users while remaining technically different from authentic brand domains. The campaigns typically promised free shoes, watches, coupons, or anniversary gifts.\n\n\nEmail, SMS, and phone scams expand the ecosystem\n\n\nSHEIN-themed email campaigns\n\nSecurity researchers also identified a large-scale email operation impersonating SHEIN order-confirmation messages.\n\nThe campaign relied on domains including:\n\n * sheinnotice[.]com\n * sheinemail[.]com\n\n\nThe attackers tried to make the messages look authentic through localized European content and retailer-themed branding. One unusual indicator involved legal documentation hosted on Google Drive rather than official websites.\n\n\nCounterfeit World Cup merchandise stores\n\nResearchers also observed counterfeit merchandise campaigns exploiting FIFA World Cup 2026 interest.\n\nAssociated domains included:\n\n * footballiscrazy[.]com\n * unitedfutballjersey[.]com\n * kicksfireshoes.com[.]co\n\n\nThe operators promoted jerseys and football merchandise through email and SMS campaigns targeting European fans.\n\n\nAmazon clone sites and subscription traps\n\nAmazon branding emerged as one of the most frequently observed in terms of impersonation attempts during the investigation.\n\nResearchers identified:\n\n * feasino[.]shop\n * amazon-clone-*.vercel[.]app\n\n\nThese operations seemed to be designed to exploit consumer trust in the Amazon brand to collect payment information and potentially enroll users in unwanted subscription schemes.\n\n\nEstablished fake-shop networks continue operating\n\n\nHomborg Online Handel runs a rotating network of German fake shops\n\nOne of the most mature operations identified during the investigation was a network operating under variations of the name “Homborg Online Handel.”\n\nThe German consumer protection association Verbraucherschutz Deutschland online e.V. has published warnings about the operation, which reportedly runs multiple professional-looking online stores that target shoppers with attractive pricing and what look like legitimate checkout experiences.\n\nAccording to the investigation, the storefronts advertise multiple payment options, including card payments and PayPal, but ultimately accept only SEPA advance bank transfers during checkout. Because bank transfers offer limited recourse once funds are sent, victims face serious challenges recovering their money.\n\nResearchers observed a pattern of rapid domain rotation. When one domain is removed or blocked, another appears shortly afterward. As of May 2026, the network included domains such as:\n\n * alpinvolt[.]de\n * kaimoro[.]de\n * havro[.]de\n * kavri[.]de\n * heyomi[.]de\n * norya[.]de\n\n\nThis extensive operation shows how fake-shop networks increasingly resemble legitimate e-commerce businesses, complete with professional storefronts, customer-service workflows, and infrastructure designed to survive enforcement efforts.\n\n\nRomanian fake-order calls\n\nAnother operation relied on telephone calls. Victims received calls claiming they had already placed an online order. Attackers pressured recipients to confirm purchases, provide personal information, or complete payments for products they never requested.\n\nThe products varied widely, from fashion items to health supplements and household goods.\n\n\nLidl-themed fake shops\n\nResearchers also found Lidl impersonation campaigns delivered through sponsored search advertisements. The fake storefronts visually mimicked Lidl branding and appeared directly within search results, making it more likely that consumers would mistake them for legitimate retailer listings.\n\n\nFake shops are becoming professionalized\n\nThe investigation shows how fake-shop operators increasingly combine paid advertising, messaging platforms, counterfeit supply chains, and localized social engineering into coordinated campaigns spanning multiple countries.\n\nRather than relying on a single fraudulent website, operators rotate domains, impersonate multiple brands simultaneously and adapt the messages to local audiences. Some campaigns focus on harvesting payments while others prioritize collecting personal information, delivery addresses or credentials. Many do both.\n\nWith FIFA World Cup 2026-related scams already emerging and several campaigns showing signs of expanding elsewhere in Europe, consumers should expect fake-shop activity to continue growing throughout 2026.\n\nThe evolution from isolated scam websites to coordinated fake-commerce ecosystems suggests that fake shops will remain one of the most persistent consumer cyberthreats in Europe.\n\n\nIndicators of compromise (IOCs)\n\n\nSamsung-related infrastructure\n\n * shopintertec[.]com\n * shop.zppzddw[.]shop\n * shop.fjjfxxz[.]shop\n * shop.pjjpjjq[.]shop\n * shop.oqqovvy[.]shop\n * shop.jrsjrsx[.]shop\n * crowndistrictstore[.]com\n\n\n\nZARA and Nike infrastructure\n\n * notcia[.]shop\n * nebularaless[.]info\n * shryxla-mynx[.]icu\n * isblkx[.]shop\n\n\n\nH&M infrastructure\n\n * cyberloria[.]cfd\n\n\n\nSHEIN infrastructure\n\n * sheinnotice[.]com\n * sheinemail[.]com\n\n\n\nAmazon impersonation infrastructure\n\n * feasino[.]shop\n * amazon-clone-*.vercel[.]app\n\n\n\nWorld Cup merchandise infrastructure\n\n * footballiscrazy[.]com\n * unitedfutballjersey[.]com\n * kicksfireshoes.com[.]co\n\n\n\nUnicode lookalike domains\n\n * adidaš[.]com\n * adidȧs[.]com\n * adidạs[.]com\n * ṇike[.]com\n * niḳe[.]com\n * rołex[.]com\n * rolẹx[.]com\n\n\n\nCounterfeit supplier infrastructure\n\n * carl-album.x.yupoo[.]com\n * adidas555.x.yupoo[.]com\n * golden-goose.x.yupoo[.]com\n * xy666999.x.yupoo[.]com\n * qiqiyg[.]com\n * bags.qiqiyg[.]com\n\n\n\nHomborg network\n\n * alpinvolt[.]de\n * kaimoro[.]de\n * havro[.]de\n * kavri[.]de\n * heyomi[.]de\n * norya[.]de\n\n\n\nAdditional fake-shop infrastructure\n\n * zarino-originals[.]com\n * newsparkings[.]com\n * love-outdoor[.]com\n * dropshipexpress.net\n * markandrebels[.]com\n * zenouv[.]com\n\n\n\nRedirect infrastructure\n\nlinkrdr[.]cc\n\ndealgo[.]cc\n\nrewardpillar[.]cc\n\nofferpilot[.]cc\n\nmsgdeal[.]cc\n\n\nThis article is published for informational and educational purposes only. Bitdefender disclaims any liability for actions taken based on the information in this article.",tags:[{id:o,name:p,slug:q,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a},{id:e,name:f,slug:c,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a},{id:m,name:g,slug:g,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a}],authors:[{id:au,name:av,slug:aw,profile_image:ax,cover_image:a,bio:ay,website:a,location:a,facebook:a,twitter:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a},{id:r,name:s,slug:t,profile_image:u,cover_image:a,bio:v,website:a,location:a,facebook:a,twitter:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a}],excerpt:D,url:az},{id:aF,title:aG,slug:aH,feature_image:aI,featured:d,published_at:aJ,custom_excerpt:a,plaintext:"Bitdefender researchers have uncovered a surge in subscription scams, both in scale and sophistication, spurred by a massive campaign involving hundreds of fraudulent websites.\n\nWhat sets this campaign apart is the significant investment cybercriminals have undertaken to make these fake sites look convincingly legitimate.  \n\nGone are the days when a suspicious email, SMS, or basic phishing link could easily fool users. As people grow more cautious and cyber-aware, scammers are stepping up their game. They have already begun crafting more complex and convincing schemes to bypass skepticism and lure victims into handing over sensitive information, especially credit card data. \n\n\nKey Findings \n\n * Incredibly convincing websites, selling everything from shoes and clothes to diverse electronics, are tricking people into paying monthly subscriptions and willingly give away credit card data. \n * Many of the websites are linked to a single address in Cyprus, likely home to an offshore company. \n * The scam encompassed more than 200 different websites, including many that are still up and running. \n * Criminals create Facebook pages and take out full ads to promote the already classic \"mystery box\" scam and other variants. \n * The \"mystery box\" scam has evolved and now includes almost hidden recurring payments, alongside links to websites to various shops.  \n * Facebook is used as the main platform for these new and enhanced mystery box scams \n\nContent creators are being impersonated to promote mystery boxes or fraudster create new pages that look a lot like the originals. \n\n\n Scammers try to take advantage of people's lack of attention \n\n\n\nScammers know that if a victim has reached the payment step, they're already convinced the scam is real. At that point, hesitation is low, and critical thinking is off.\n\nThat’s when scammers strike again, slipping in a second scam right before the victim hands over the money. It’s not just about closing the deal at that point, but rather about stacking the fraud.. \n\n\nWhat is a mystery box scam? \n\n\n\nIn real life, the allure of a mysterious box of items on a shelf just waiting for someone to pick it up for a few bucks seems like a scam that would never work. But on the Internet, it really does work - otherwise scammers wouldn't put so much effort into promoting them. \n\nThere are quite a few variations of these scams, from boxes left at the post office to bags left at the airport and even to clearance sales from large shopping centers. They all share the same tell-tale sign: all the victim has to do is to pay a minimal sum of money. \n\nThe goal, of course, is to collect personal and financial information. Victims willingly provide all that precious information, believing they've made a fantastic purchase. Here's an example from one such scam campaign targeting Facebook users in Romania:  \n\n\nThe Mystery Box Scam is evolving \n\n\n\nLike most scams, these fraudulent schemes lower their allure as people get used to them, and fewer people fall victim. This drives criminals to devise new ways to obtain money or financial information. \n\nThe first step in this evolutionary ladder was the moment scammers added surveys \"to ensure\" you're a real person and not a bot. When users see a company taking such steps, it makes the enterprise look more legitimate.  \n\nNow, the mystery box scam has evolved in a new way. Right before you agree to give them money and financial information, you also agree to a subscription model (written in a tiny font) that turns your current mystery shopping adventure into recurring payments. \n\n \n\n Of course, other countries are targeted as well. Here's one for Canada or the United States:  \n\nAs our past research shows, these scams have flooded social media, and it's all made possible by sponsored ads. \n\nYou will notice that the payment page also references a website called naillr[.]com, where you get a loyalty membership card that gives you discounts and perks. However, this is where the research pointed us in another direction. \n\n\nThe mystery box scam is expanding into new territories \n\n\n\nSome of these ads with mystery boxes point to various online shops for a variety of products, like clothes, electronic equipment, beauty products, and many others. At one point, we identified around 140 websites that shared the same business model. This is just one example: \n\n\"Buy at member price and get FREE access to the best prices in Europe with an account top-up of 44.00 EUR\u002Fevery 14 days. Skip or shop the top-up\" read the fine print.  \n\nThe online shop appears to offer many tiers with all kinds of perks. By following the URLs related by tracker ID, Bitdefender researchers found more than 200 websites in this campaign, many of which are currently still online. \n\nBasically, people might be tempted to pay one of these subscriptions, believing that it will provide them with discounts across the entire website. The shop owners even offer various subscription tiers, but the sums vary from one website to another.  \n\nThis is what the VIP tier looks for on one of these websites:  \n\n \n\nThe discounts offered are based on store credits, which are transformed using a 1:1 ratio. So if you invest €68 you get 68 credits. If you want to buy something like a piece of furniture, for example, this is what it would look like. \n\n \n\nIt's all very complicated to follow, with store credits, discounts, credits that you can top up every 14 days, and so on. The basic idea is to have a process as convoluted as possible and make it sound like a good idea at the same time. By the time the victim actually pays for a subscription, it already seems like an investment. \n\nThey often promise all the best products money can buy, but their offers are ridiculous. This one electronic store sold old cables, obsolete technologies, and other devices that could be bought for a fraction of the price from Chinese stores. \n\nIt's also important to mention that the contact address mentioned in most of these hundreds of websites (Andrea Kalvou 13, 3085 Limassol) that are still up and running also appears in conjunction with a Cypryorecord in the International Consortium of Investigative Journalists (ICIJ) Offshore Leaks Database that is associated with the Paradise Papers leak. \n\n\nThe subscription allure is too strong \n\n\n\nCriminals have been pumping funds in ads promoting impersonated content creators, using the same subscription model that seems to be now the driving revenue stream of these scams.  \n\nScammers often change the impersonated brands, and they've begun expanding past the existing mystery boxes. They are now trying to sell low-quality products or imitation articles, fake investments, supplements, and much more.  \n\nWe have observed several techniques used to evade automatic detection: \n\n * Multiple versions of the ad, with only one being malicious, while the others display random product images. \n * Uploading images directly from Google Drive (so they can be replaced later). \n * Using cropped images to alter visual patterns. \n * Relying exclusively on images in ads, with no text in the description (text appears only in the image itself). \n * Classic homoglyph techniques. \n\nSome of these account pages can be created from scratch with names generated by algorithms, or they've been hacked and taken over, after which they've been renamed.    \n\n \n\nThese stores might not seem to have anything in common, but for the most part, they use the same design, the same themes, the same AI agents, and similar registration information, pointing to Cyprus.  \n\nWhile it's difficult to make a direct connection between Mystery Box Scams and this swarm of websites, the fact that the payment screens for some Mystery Boxes have links to Cyprus-registered subscription-based shops is suspicious, to say the least, especially when these scams share the same subscription idea. \n\n\n Conclusion \n\nWhile many of these frauds are seemingly linked to the same operators, a lot of other scammers also figure out that subscription is the new normal.\n\nWith funds pumped into ads, real-looking websites, impersonations of people and brands, and all kinds of other avenues of attack, we're bound to see these kind of frauds inundate the online world. \n\nIOC:\n\n'bestsoundclub[.]com',\n\n 'egadgets[.]club',\n\n 'betrendy[.]site',\n\n 'allbuysport[.]com',\n\n 'alltv[.]store',\n\n 'allgamesinone[.]com',\n\n 'allmakeup[.]vip',\n\n 'dom.attentiontamers[.]com',\n\n 'bol.bluedeutsch[.]com',\n\n 'd.strideoflife[.]com',\n\n 'bestkitchens[.]store',\n\n 'aul.hyperhorizon[.]org',\n\n 'click.purebudgets100[.]com',\n\n 'decorstore[.]club',\n\n 'amazitech[.]com',\n\n 'decorhome[.]vip',\n\n 'abeautybuffet[.]com',\n\n 'buygadgets[.]site',\n\n 'decorsolutions[.]online',\n\n 'devicesair[.]com',\n\n 'aul.honeymusic[.]org',\n\n 'allfree[.]me',\n\n 'aul.stellafromvalhalla[.]org',\n\n 'dealmeon[.]club',\n\n 'clicks.dyslexicsonfire[.]com',\n\n 'aul.intothestory[.]org',\n\n 'alljewellery[.]store',\n\n 'cosykitchen[.]store',\n\n 'apps.risetotheoccasions[.]org',\n\n 'accelredirect[.]com',\n\n 'clicks.ahauntedcastleuponahill[.]com',\n\n 'dom.highoctaneavenger[.]com',\n\n 'afterhourshobby[.]com',\n\n 'click.maestrolanding[.]com',\n\n 'beoutdoors[.]site',\n\n 'beachitem[.]com',\n\n 'brandclothesshop[.]site',\n\n 'bestcosmetic[.]club',\n\n 'best-dealclub[.]com',\n\n 'email.all4tech[.]site',\n\n 'clicks.sightseeingdragon[.]com',\n\n 'bestlook[.]store',\n\n 'alltoys[.]store',\n\n 'altaprotect[.]com',\n\n 'beoutdoors[.]club',\n\n 'decorworld[.]shop',\n\n 'clicks.feelthesummervibe[.]com',\n\n 'designerhome[.]store',\n\n '0dgwn.bemobtrcks[.]com',\n\n 'aul.twotearsandamoon[.]com',\n\n 'aul.magneticstormcatcher[.]com',\n\n 'cookskitchen[.]club',\n\n 'clicks.offtheworld[.]org',\n\n 'bestylish[.]club',\n\n 'cosmeticshop[.]store',\n\n 'clicks.hellobatchsix[.]com',\n\n 'craftcraze[.]shop',\n\n 'coolgarden[.]club',\n\n 'd.snowflakepipeline[.]com',\n\n 'clickgadgets[.]club',\n\n 'agamingportal[.]com',\n\n 'bestclothes[.]club',\n\n 'alldaysgift[.]co',\n\n 'cooltv[.]shop',\n\n 'allyours[.]vip',\n\n 'clotheszone[.]club',\n\n 'beauty365[.]site',\n\n 'daysgiftrewards[.]com',\n\n 'clicks.stalingradrailstation[.]com',\n\n 'belovedaroma[.]com',\n\n 'elect 9 Update:\n\nThe oldest sample we were able to track until now (e69b50d1d58056fc770c88c514af9a82) shows the malware during its early development stage. Dated 2023-04-12, it looks like a Stage 2 sample with the C&C address set to 127.0.0.1, which leads us to believe that it was used for testing. It also includes limited functionality that currently is available in Stage 3 samples (only listening for OS commands (executed with  exec) ), which reinforces our assumption that the malware was in development at that time.\n\nWe also noticed that the Java package was named differently back in April ( dev.sirlennox.nekoclient  instead of  dev.neko.nekoclient).\n\nWe also identified several executables (NekoInstaller\u002FNekoService), as described below:\n\n * NekoInstaller contains a Portable Executable file named NekoServices; at execution it drops the executable file in  C:\\Program Files\\nekoservice\\ServiceHost.exe  and adds it as a service named  NekoService  to run;\n * NekoService contains a JAR file as a resource; it will download a JRE and run the JAR file; the JAR file is then dropped in  C:\\Program Files\\nekoservice\\service.jar\n * the JAR file seems to be a Stage 2 sample\n * the MZ header compilation time for most of these samples ranges between 2023-04-20 18:20:06 and 2023-05-01 22:17:25, and just one sample was identified to be compiled on 2023-05-21 07:58:12, which means that the malware was in development in that timeframe.\n * Based on this evidence, it seems that the attackers initially planned to distribute EXE files rather than JAR ones, but we were unable to assess whether the EXE files have been disseminated in the wild or not.\n\nInitial article:\n\nSeveral Minecraft mods and plugins hosted on the CurseForge and Bukkit modding communities have been tainted with a multi-stage, multi-platform infostealer malware called Fractureiser, a preliminary investigation shows.\n\nSeveral CurseForge and Bukkit accounts have been compromised and used to publish malware-rigged updates of mods and plugins without the knowledge of the original author. These mods have trickled downstream into popular modpacks that have been downloaded several million times to date.\n\nThe malware has 4 stages, labeled 0 through 3. Stage 0 is considered the modified mod or plugin to include obfuscated code that connects to http:\u002F\u002F85.217.144.130:8080\u002Fdl to download the Stage 1 malware.\n\nThe Stage 1 malware comes in the form of a dl.jar file with a SHA-1 sum of dc43c4685c3f47808ac207d1667cc1eb915b2d82. The Stage 1 malware includes a mutex to prevent it from running multiple times, and it seems responsible for infecting other JAR files, establishing persistence and contacting the command and control server in preparation of Stage 2 deployment.\n\nStage 2 (lib.jar or libWebGL64.jar) acts as a downloader and updater for the final payload in Stage 3.\n\nStage 3 brings the final payload, in the form of a jar file that includes a native binary named hook.dll. Hook.dll is exposing two functionalities that are called from Java code: retrieveClipboardFiles - to retrieve file descriptors from the clipboard, used for the virtual machine escape technique (detailed below), as well as retrieveMSACredentials to retrieve Microsoft Live credentials.\n\n\nWhat we know so far\n\nThe first sample apparently dates all the way back to April 24th 2023 in the form of a Stage 0 malware with the 0e583c572ad823330b9e34d871fcc2df hash. The first JAR (Java Archive) file lacks many of the features currently in the malware.\n\nThe malware currently affects Linux and Windows Minecraft installs and attempts to inject itself into all other eligible .jar files on the system, including those that are not part of a Minecraft mod. The malware has a complex logic to determine whether a .jar file is a candidate for infection. Upon modification of the file, the infection code also disables code signing for Java files by removing the META-INF\u002FCERTIFIC.RSA, META-INF\u002FCERTIFIC.EC, META-INF\u002FCERT.SF and META-INF\u002FCERTIFIC.SF\n\nThe malware monitors the clipboard for crypto-currency wallet addresses, then swaps them with the attacker’s to hijack transactions. It also steals Minecraft and Discord authentication tokens, as well as cookies and login data stored in the most popular browsers.\n\nDuring our analysis, we identified interesting behavior we believe is aimed at mod or plugin developers. It looks like the Stage 3 malware targets Windows Sandbox instances used for testing mods by monitoring and constantly poisoning the clipboard in an attempt to infect the host. This behavior is isolated to Windows Sandbox, as it is the only virtualization environment that allows alteration of the host clipboard contents when the virtual machine is running in the background.\n\nWe were able to confirm that dozens of mods and plugins have been rigged with the malware. The affected mods are listed in the Indicators of Compromise section below.\n\nThe overwhelming majority of victims are in the US. We are monitoring the individual components of this malware and will update the threat distribution accordingly.\n\n\nMitigation\n\nBitdefender identifies the malicious code in all stages of execution as Trojan.Java.Fractureiser.*. If you have downloaded any of the infected mods in recent months or have any concern about the integrity of your .jar files, run a deep scan with your favorite security solution such as Bitdefender Total Security.\n\n\nIndicators of compromise\n\nFiles\n\n\n\n\nSHA-1\nDetection\n\n\n\n\n2db855a7f40c015f8c9ca7cbab69e1f1aafa210b\nTrojan.Java.Fractureiser.B, Exploitation Hits the Smart Home",slug:"cve-2025-55182-exploitation-hits-the-smart-home",feature_image:"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2025\u002F12\u002Fpexels-jakubzerdzicki-19089175.jpg",featured:d,published_at:"2025-12-09T12:37:00.000+03:00",custom_excerpt:a,html:"\u003Cp\u003EShortly after details of \u003Ccode\u003ECVE-2025-55182\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E became public, we began noticing large volumes of exploitation attempts across our endpoint and network sensors. The vulnerability, informally referred to as React2Shell, affects Node.js applications that allow user-supplied JSON data to influence internal JavaScript object structures. When improperly validated, attackers can escalate this into remote command execution through access to \u003Ccode\u003Eprocess.mainModule.require\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E&nbsp;and, subsequently, \u003Ccode\u003Echild_process.execSync\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E. For a complete overview of the vulnerability, we have&nbsp;\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fblog\u002Fbusinessinsights\u002Fadvisory-react2shell-critical-unauthenticated-rce-in-react-cve-2025-55182\"\u003E\u003Cu\u003Ethe deep-dive blog post on Business Insights\u003C\u002Fu\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBecause this vulnerability is simple to weaponize and widely applicable, it was rapidly adopted by botnet operators. Smart home insights for the past 30 days provide a clear picture of how the exploit is being used in active campaigns.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"scale-of-the-attacks\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EScale of the attacks\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe recorded more than 150,000 blocked exploit attempts matching the React2Shell pattern per day. The majority of detections involved direct command injection signatures linked to \u003Ccode\u003EBusyBox\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E  execution, file download attempts via \u003Ccode\u003Ewget\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E or \u003Ccode\u003Ecurl\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, privilege modification through \u003Ccode\u003Echmod\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, and various obfuscation patterns (including base64 decoding sequences designed to evade simple filtering).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;While some requests were reconnaissance probes, most were structured payloads intended to download and run malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"attack-origin\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EAttack origin\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA significant portion of the traffic originates from a datacenter in Poland. One IP address in particular was responsible for more than 12,000 React2Shell-related events, along with port scanning and attempts to exploit known Hikvision vulnerabilities. This behavior aligns with patterns seen in Mirai-derived botnets, where compromised infrastructure is used both for scanning and for launching multi-vector attacks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAdditional probing comes from the United States, the Netherlands, Ireland, France, Hong Kong, Singapore, China, Panama, and other regions, indicating broad global participation in opportunistic exploitation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch1 id=\"targeted-devices\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ETargeted devices\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fh1\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAttack attempts were directed at a wide variety of device types, showing the opportunistic nature of such attempts. The most frequently targeted models included smart plugs, smart phones, NAS devices, surveillance systems, routers, development boards, as well as various makes and models of smart TVs and consumer electronics.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe large number of \u003Ccode\u003Eunknown\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E device fingerprints suggests many attacks targeted generic Linux-based web interfaces that do not expose clear identification data. This spread matches established botnet targeting behavior: attackers probe any device with an exposed HTTP endpoint, regardless of manufacturer or purpose.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EPayload analysis\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe identified two major payload families delivered through React2Shell in the last week:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Col\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EMirai and Mirai-derived loaders.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003Cbr\u003EThese payloads often used \u003Ccode\u003EBusyBox\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E commands to download binaries from infrastructure hosted at \u003Ccode\u003E193.34.213[.]150\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, using filenames such as \u003Ccode\u003Ex86\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E and \u003Ccode\u003Ebolts\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E. The infection chain included download, permission modification, execution, and a follow-up request to retrieve secondary components.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ERondo miner deployments.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003Cbr\u003EAnother campaign used the vulnerability to download the script&nbsp;\u003Ccode\u003Erondo.aqu.sh\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E&nbsp;from \u003Ccode\u003E41.231.37[.]153\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E. This installer fetched both a propagation module and a cryptocurrency mining component.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Fol\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBoth types of campaigns align with typical botnet monetization strategies: distributed denial-of-service capability, further worming, and illicit mining.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhy React2Shell is being widely used\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Ccode\u003ECVE-2025-55182\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E offers attackers a straightforward path from web request to system-level command execution. The exploit payload is compact, requires no advanced techniques, and works against a large class of Node.js applications. Botnet operators historically adopt vulnerabilities like this within days, and our monitoring shows that React2Shell is no exception.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat this means for users\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe exploitation attempts we observed are automated and indiscriminate. Any publicly exposed service running a vulnerable implementation is a potential target. Organizations that develop or deploy Node.js applications should apply available patches immediately and verify that JSON parsing logic does not allow prototype pollution or object structure manipulation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFor IoT and consumer devices, reducing exposure through proper network segmentation and disabling unnecessary remote access remains essential. Once a device is compromised, it is commonly repurposed as an attack platform, perpetuating the cycle of scanning and exploitation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe will continue monitoring activity related to \u003Ccode\u003ECVE-2025-55182\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E and provide updates as new payloads or threat actors emerge.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"appendix-indicators-of-compromise-iocs\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EAppendix: Indicators of Compromise (IoCs)\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"malicious-infrastructure\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EMalicious infrastructure\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EPrimary exploit and malware delivery hosts observed in React2Shell campaigns:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E\u003Ccode\u003E193.34.213.150\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E-&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EUsed to deliver Mirai-style binaries under paths including&nbsp;\u003Ccode\u003E\u002Fnuts\u002Fx86\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E&nbsp;and&nbsp;\u003Ccode\u003E\u002Fnuts\u002Fbolts\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E. ",reading_time:bw,url:"\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Fcve-2025-55182-exploitation-hits-the-smart-home\u002F"},{id:"688a2c7495161a04e7e9533a",title:"Vulnerabilities Identified in Dahua Hero C1 Smart Cameras",slug:"vulnerabilities-identified-in-dahua-hero-c1-smart-cameras",feature_image:"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2025\u002F07\u002FTechnology_Background_-_Digital_Hand_Unlocking_Portal-1.jpg",featured:d,published_at:"2025-07-30T17:00:00.000+03:00",custom_excerpt:a,html:"\u003Cp\u003EResearchers at Bitdefender have identified critical security vulnerabilities in the firmware of the&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003EDahua Hero C1 (DH-H4C)\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;smart camera series. The flaws, affecting the device's ONVIF protocol and file upload handlers, allow unauthenticated attackers to execute arbitrary commands remotely, effectively taking over the device.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe vulnerabilities were reported to Dahua for responsible mitigation and disclosure and are now patched at the time of publication.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"affected-devices\"\u003EAffected Devices\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe issues were verified on a&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003EDahua Hero C1 (DH-H4C)\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;running firmware version&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003EV2.810.9992002.0.R (Build Date: 2024-01-23)\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;with&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003EONVIF version 21.06\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;and&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003EWeb UI version V3.2.1.1452137\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. This version was confirmed as the latest available when starting our research through the device’s own update interface.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOther device models that were identified during the vendor’s own audit include: IPC-1XXX Series, IPC-2XXX Series, IPC-WX Series, IPC-ECXX Series, SD3A Series, SD2A Series, SD3D Series, SDT2A Series, SD2C Series with firmware versions older than 2025\u002F04\u002F16. \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"acknowledgement\"\u003EAcknowledgement\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;We would like to extend our sincere thanks to the Dahua security team for their professional handling of the vulnerabilities reported. Their prompt triage, prioritization, and resolution of the issues demonstrate a strong commitment to customer safety and product integrity. This type of collaboration between researchers and vendors is extremely valuable to the broader cybersecurity ecosystem - ensuring that vulnerabilities are addressed before they can be weaponized. We hope to see this level of responsiveness and transparency replicated across the industry.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"disclosure-timeline\"\u003EDisclosure Timeline\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMar 28, 2025: Bitdefender shares the findings with the Dahua team through a secure communication channel\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMar 29, 2025: Dahua acknowledges reception and proceeds with internal investigation&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EApr 01, 2025: Dahua confirms the reports as valid&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EApr 23, 2025: Dahua asks for an extension, Bitdefender moves disclosure timeframe for Jul 23\u003Csup\u003Erd\u003C\u002Fsup\u003E.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJul 07, 2025: Dahua releases patches for the vulnerability and confirms the Jul 30th&nbsp;coordinated disclosure\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJul 30, 2025: This report becomes public as part of the coordinated responsible disclosure efforts\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"vulnerability-1-stack-based-buffer-overflow-in-onvif-protocol-handler-cve-2025-31700\"\u003EVulnerability 1: Stack-Based Buffer Overflow in ONVIF Protocol Handler (\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2025-31700\" rel=\"noreferrer\"\u003ECVE-2025-31700\u003C\u002Fa\u003E)\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe ONVIF request handler on port 80 (function at 0x19e530) parses the Host header incorrectly, leading to an unbounded copy of the header on the stack. If the header contains a ‘]’ character that is not followed by a ‘:’ character, the strncpy function gets the size by subtracting the buffer’s address from the address of the ‘]’ character. This allows an attacker to write an arbitrary number of bytes to the stack, as long as the payload does not contain a ‘]’ character or a null byte. This oversight allows for an unauthenticated stack-based buffer overflow, ultimately overwriting the return address and several CPU registers (r4–r11).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EThe strncpy call at 0x0019e898:\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-59b8454b-3bf5-4103-9207-ff725d05a12d.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"A screenshot of a computer code\n\nAI-generated content may be incorrect.\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"634\" height=\"471\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-59b8454b-3bf5-4103-9207-ff725d05a12d.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-59b8454b-3bf5-4103-9207-ff725d05a12d.png 634w\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EExploitation is possible without prior authentication, and a successful attack results in full code execution. The proof-of-concept (PoC) developed by researchers shows the attacker writing system commands into memory and then invoking them through carefully crafted return-oriented programming (ROP) chains. The PoC drops an ELF payload using&nbsp;tftp&nbsp;and spawns a bind shell on port 4444 using&nbsp;LD_PRELOAD, bypassing binary signature checks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-d0a03cad-fe51-4274-8fd4-51f03c4d90f3.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"A close up of a computer screen\n\nAI-generated content may be incorrect.\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"855\" height=\"169\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-d0a03cad-fe51-4274-8fd4-51f03c4d90f3.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-d0a03cad-fe51-4274-8fd4-51f03c4d90f3.png 855w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Chr\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"vulnerability-2-bss-segment-overflow-via-rpc-upload-handler-cve-2025-31701\"\u003EVulnerability 2: .bss Segment Overflow via RPC Upload Handler (\u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.cve.org\u002FCVERecord?id=CVE-2025-31701\" rel=\"noreferrer\"\u003ECVE-2025-31701\u003C\u002Fa\u003E)\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA second vulnerability exists in the handler for the undocumented endpoint&nbsp;POST \u002FRPC2_UploadFileWithName\u002F*. Here, the camera copies the&nbsp;Cseq&nbsp;HTTP header directly into a buffer located in the&nbsp;.bss&nbsp;memory section using a flawed&nbsp;strncpy&nbsp;implementation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003Estrncpy&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003Ecall at 0x00583948:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-d2db59c6-8497-4890-a222-84d084011976.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"A screenshot of a computer program\n\nAI-generated content may be incorrect.\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"641\" height=\"179\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-d2db59c6-8497-4890-a222-84d084011976.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2025\u002F07\u002Fdata-src-image-d2db59c6-8497-4890-a222-84d084011976.png 641w\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe variable at 0x03955c38 stores a pointer to a structure that can be overwritten by&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003Estrncpy\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. The functions at 0x00586dd4 and 0x00586d18 continuously check this structure for expired sessions. Because no bounds checking is performed, an attacker can&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003Eoverwrite adjacent global variables\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, including a structure that stores pointers to session management functions. These are routinely invoked by the firmware to manage timeouts. By planting a crafted structure in memory, the attacker can redirect execution to a call to&nbsp;system(), again resulting in full remote code execution—no authentication needed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFunction 0x00586dd4 using a function pointer from the controlled structure:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg code with many colored text\n\nAI-generated content may be incorrect.\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"695\" height=\"205\" 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"security-impact\"\u003ESecurity Impact\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBoth vulnerabilities are unauthenticated and exploitable over the local network. Devices exposed to the internet through port forwarding or UPnP are especially at risk. Successful exploitation provides root-level access to the camera with no user interaction.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBecause the exploit path bypasses firmware integrity checks, attackers can load unsigned payloads or persist via custom daemons, making cleanup difficult.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"recommendations\"\u003E&nbsp;Recommendations\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EUsers should avoid exposing the Dahua camera web interface of vulnerable models to the internet.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;Disable UPnP and remove port forwarding rules if present.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EIsolate the camera on a separate VLAN\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;or dedicated IoT network to limit lateral movement.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EMonitor vendor updates\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;and apply patches as soon as available. As of writing, firmware versions after 2025\u002F04\u002F16 fix the issues.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E",tags:[{id:e,name:f,slug:c,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a},{id:U,name:A,slug:R,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a},{id:J,name:K,slug:L,description:a,feature_image:a,visibility:b,og_image:a,og_title:a,og_description:a,twitter_image:a,twitter_title:a,twitter_description:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a,codeinjection_head:a,codeinjection_foot:a,canonical_url:a,accent_color:a}],authors:[{id:E,name:F,slug:G,profile_image:H,cover_image:a,bio:I,website:a,location:a,facebook:a,twitter:a,meta_title:a,meta_description:a}],excerpt:"Researchers at Bitdefender have identified critical security vulnerabilities in the firmware of the Dahua Hero C1 (DH-H4C) smart camera series. The flaws, affecting the device's ONVIF protocol and file upload handlers, allow unauthenticated attackers to execute arbitrary commands remotely, effectively taking over the device.\n\nThe vulnerabilities were reported to Dahua for responsible mitigation and disclosure and are now patched at the time of publication.\n\n\nAffected Devices\n\nThe issues were ver",reading_time:bw,url:"\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Fvulnerabilities-identified-in-dahua-hero-c1-smart-cameras\u002F"},{id:"66d5c3d2d0ec1aefc7d722d2",title:"60 Hurts per Second – How We Got Access to Enough Solar Power to Run the United States",slug:"60-hurts-per-second-how-we-got-access-to-enough-solar-power-to-run-the-united-states",feature_image:"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2024\u002F08\u002F1ZZ96uESRJQ.jpg",featured:d,published_at:"2024-08-07T16:16:00.000+03:00",custom_excerpt:a,html:"\u003Cp\u003EThe electricity grid – the buzzing, crackling marvel that supplies the lifeblood of modernity - is by far the largest structure humanity ever built. It’s so big, in fact, that few people even notice it, like a fish can’t see the ocean.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUntil the grid goes down, that is. Then, like the fish dangling from the angler’s hook, we see our vulnerability. Modernity dissolves into a sudden silence, followed by the repeated flick of a light switch, and a howl of panic at the prospect of missed appointments, ruined dinners, lost workdays, stopped elevators and dark, cold evenings, and worse.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBut most of the time, the grid hums along just fine ...\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENow, take that labyrinthine system and add another complex network, this one delivering hundreds of gigawatts of power from the sun - solar power infrastructure. This ever-fluctuating source of energy is tapped via rooftop panels, distant solar farms, slapdash home arrays and other evolving infrastructure around the globe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnd with complexity comes risk ...\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThen, take those two systems and intersect them with the Internet of Things, an ethereal network that runs from the limits of human thought – both positive and malicious – into the routines of daily life through seemingly innocuous gadgets.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt that intersection we find the mundane world of inverters and controllers.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnd that's where a group of researchers stumbled upon an alarming fact:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003Ea solar grid responsible for 195 gigawatts, or 20 percent of the world’s solar power output, is vulnerable. That’s enough to power the entire United States - and it’s just waiting to be hijacked.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-findings\"\u003EKey Findings\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EBitdefender researchers have identified a series of vulnerabilities in PV plant management platforms operated by Solarman and Deye.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThis platform is responsible for coordinating production operations of millions of solar installations worldwide generating a whopping output of approximately 195 GW of solar power (20% of the global solar production)\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EIf exploited, these vulnerabilities could allow an attacker to control inverter settings that could take parts of the grid down, potentially causing blackouts.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EThese vulnerabilities have been communicated to the affected vendors and fixed.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"understanding-the-power-grid\"\u003EUnderstanding the Power Grid\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe power grid is a vast network of power plants, transmission lines and distribution systems that delivers electricity from producers to consumers. It consists of three main components:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Col\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EGeneration\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Power plants generate electricity using various sources, including coal, natural gas, nuclear, hydro and, increasingly, solar and wind.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ETransmission\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: High-voltage transmission lines carry electricity over long distances from power plants to substations.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EDistribution\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Substations lower the voltage for safe distribution to homes and businesses through local power lines.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Fol\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe grid balances supply and demand in real time, ensuring that electricity is available when needed. When generation exceeds demand, voltage in the grid rises to dangerous levels that can damage sensitive equipment connected to it.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhen generation can't meet demand, anomalies begin to occur. Initially, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Farstechnica.com\u002Ftech-policy\u002F2018\u002F03\u002Fovens-across-europe-display-the-wrong-time-due-to-a-serbia-kosovo-grid-dispute\u002F\"\u003Eyour microwave oven may get several minutes slower\u003C\u002Fa\u003E (as it computes its current time based on the “ticks” in the network measured in Hertz: 60 Hertz - or cycles per second - in the US, and 50 Hz in Europe). Secondly, parts of the grid where the imbalance is detected are disconnected before they drag the whole system into a downwards spiral that can take days to recover from.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2024\u002F08\u002Fimage.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt loading=\"lazy\" width=\"904\" height=\"500\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2024\u002F08\u002Fimage.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2024\u002F08\u002Fimage.png 904w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFascinated? To learn more about how the grid works, you might want to watch \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.youtube.com\u002Fwatch?v=v1BMWczn7JM\"\u003Ethis episode of Practical Engineering\u003C\u002Fa\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"the-role-of-solar-energy-in-the-grid\"\u003EThe Role of Solar Energy in the Grid\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESolar energy has a powerful impact on the energy landscape. Solar panels convert sunlight into a clean, renewable source of power, which can be used immediately, stored in batteries, or fed into the grid.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESolar power systems are often decentralized, meaning they are installed on individual homes or businesses rather than centralized power plants. This means that generation and distribution often take place in the same ZIP code and (with some exceptions such as solar power production in parks), disruptions at this level have a limited geographic impact.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis decentralization presents several challenges and opportunities for the grid. Solar power reduces dependence on fossil fuels, lowers greenhouse gas emissions, and can enhance grid resilience by distributing power generation. However, integrating numerous small-scale solar systems into the grid can complicate management.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESolar setups produce variable amounts of electricity, and only during daytime.  Maintaining voltage levels and managing the variability of solar power must  be outsourced to a pre-programmed critical component called an inverter. An inverter converts the direct current (DC) electricity generated by solar panels into alternating current (AC) electricity, the standard used by most homes and businesses, as well as for transmission over the power grid.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe inverter performs two primary functions in a solar power system:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EDC to AC Conversion\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Solar panels generate DC electricity  however, most electrical appliances and the power grid operate on AC electricity, which alternates in polarity and voltage. The inverter converts the DC electricity into AC electricity to  be used by household appliances or fed into the grid.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EGrid Synchronization\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: In grid-tied solar power systems, the inverter  synchronizes the phase and frequency of the AC output with the grid. This ensures that the electricity generated by the solar panels is compatible with the grid and can be safely exported to it. Differences in phase and voltage can crash the grid.  Power distributors and governments  see any deliberate attempts to bypass these grid safety measures as a threat to national security.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"security-flaws-in-solar-management-platforms\"\u003ESecurity Flaws in Solar Management Platforms\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender researchers  started looking into potential cybercriminal use of  inverters, as they are becoming increasingly visible on the radar of internet-connected devices. By the foresight of “we had one already available and we wanted to test it,” we  looked into data loggers made by Solarman that are a popular choice for monitoring Deye inverter units..\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo add context, Solarman is one of the world’s largest photovoltaic (PV) monitoring and management platform, and equipment connected to this platform is apparently responsible for the production of over \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.solarmanpv.com\u002Fcorporate\u002Fabout-us\u002F\"\u003E195 gigawatts of power across 2M+ active PV plants involving \"10M+ devices in 190+ countries and territories”.\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA quick look at the platform shows Solarman is not only an equipment builder,  they also license parts of their infrastructure to other manufacturers (see the Appendix). Our analysis shows that the Solarman API architecture has multiple entry points exposed for multiple companies selling inverters, data loggers and a wide range of PV equipment. The investigation of the Deye inverter and Solarman data logger have uncovered severe security vulnerabilities that could be exploited to disrupt or even take down the grid.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003ENote - a data logger in an inverter setup records and transmits data on the solar power system's performance. It enables real-time and historical monitoring, fault detection, and remote management, ensuring the system operates efficiently and reliably. By gathering metrics such as power output, voltage and current, the data logger helps optimize energy production and usage, facilitates maintenance through timely alerts, and integrates with cloud platforms for accessible remote monitoring. This management feature improves system performance, enhances reliability, and supports informed decision-making, ultimately contributing to the longevity and effectiveness of the solar power installation.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe Solarman platform is linked to a variety of equipment and brands revolving around it. Many of these customers seem to use the entire platform as a service, while others wrap API endpoints in custom implementations and keep customer databases private.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBeyond the multiple entry-points exposed by the platform (discussed above), we sought to discover how various parts of this architecture fit together. To validate our assumptions, we successfully attempted to register a Solarman data logger into the Deye platform.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAs far as we are able to tell, Deye has been using the original Solarman infrastructure up until 2024, but they \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.deyeinverter.com\u002Fnews\u002Fcompany-news\u002Fdeye-cloud-account-transfer.html\"\u003Ehave customized their implementation\u003C\u002Fa\u003E and spun off a new datacenter to accommodate their own user base.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe Solarman platform has two types of accounts: one is regular reporting, where the logged-in user has access to production and usage data in real time, while the other is a business (contractor) account used by the authorized installation company to control devices, modify voltage or frequency and control other power grid injection settings). With these details out of the way, it’s time to dig into how we managed to gain access to all business and regular accounts in all solar production devices made by Solarman and its partners.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"vulnerabilities-in-the-solarman-platform\"\u003EVulnerabilities in the Solarman platform\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur research on the Solarman platform uncovered the following vulnerabilities: (These flaws are detailed in a separate paper available below.)\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-card kg-file-card kg-file-card-medium\"\u003E\n            \u003Ca class=\"kg-file-card-container\" href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Ffiles\u002F2024\u002F08\u002FBitdefender-PReport-solarman-creat7907.pdf\" title=\"Download\" download\u003E\n                \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-contents\"\u003E\n                    \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-title\"\u003EVulnerabilities in the Solarman platform\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                    \n                    \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-metadata\"\u003E\n                        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-filename\"\u003EBitdefender-PReport-solarman-creat7907.pdf\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-filesize\"\u003E974 xmlns=\"http:\u002F\u002Fwww.w3.org\u002F2000\u002Fsvg\" viewBox=\"0 0 24 24\"\u003E\u003Cdefs\u003E\u003Cstyle\u003E.a{fill:none;stroke:currentColor;stroke-linecap:round;stroke-linejoin:round;stroke-width:1.5px;}\u003C\u002Fstyle\u003E\u003C\u002Fdefs\u003E\u003Ctitle\u003Edownload-circle\u003C\u002Ftitle\u003E\u003Cpolyline class=\"a\" points=\"8.25 14.25 12 18 15.75 14.25\"\u002F\u003E\u003Cline class=\"a\" x1=\"12\" y1=\"6.75\" x2=\"12\" y2=\"18\"\u002F\u003E\u003Ccircle class=\"a\" cx=\"12\" cy=\"12\" r=\"11.25\"\u002F\u003E\u003C\u002Fsvg\u003E\n                \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n            \u003C\u002Fa\u003E\n        \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n        \u003Col\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EFull Account Takeover\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The Solarman platform’s \u003Ccode\u003E\u002Foauth2-s\u002Foauth\u002Ftoken\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E API endpoint lets attackers generate authorization tokens for any account. By modifying the JSON payload, attackers can gain control over any regular or business account. This means that a malicious user could iterate through all accounts, take over any of them and modify inverter parameters or change the way the inverter interacts with the grid.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EToken Reuse Across Platforms\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: JWT tokens issued by the Deye Cloud platform are also valid on the Solarman platform, granting full access to the accounts based on their ID. This cross-platform vulnerability can lead to widespread unauthorized access in the absence of well-defined boundaries to isolate vendors.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EExcessive Data Exposure\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The Solarman platform’s API endpoints return excessive information about organizations, including private details such as email addresses and phone numbers. This information can be harvested to exploit the platform’s users and organizations. In addition to personally identifiable information, an attacker can chain this with the first vulnerability to run subsequent queries on the account and obtain GPS coordinates for solar installations as well as their real-time production capacity.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Fol\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"vulnerabilities-in-the-deye-platform\"\u003EVulnerabilities in the Deye platform\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Col\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EHard-Coded Credentials\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The Deye platform uses a hard-coded account (`SmartConfigurator@solarmanpv.com` with password \u003Ccode\u003E123456\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E) to access device data. This account can obtain an authorization token that grants access to any device, exposing sensitive information such as software versions, Wi-Fi credentials, and more​ (see the Deye disclosure report available below)​.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EInformation Leakage\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The API endpoint \u003Ccode\u003E\u002Fuser-s\u002Facc\u002Forgs\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E on eu1.deyecloud.com returns excessive private information about users, including names, email addresses, phone numbers and user IDs. This endpoint can be exploited to leak information about all users on the platform​.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EAuthorization Token Generation\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: The \u003Ccode\u003E\u002Foauth-s\u002Foauth\u002F\u003C\u002Fcode\u003Etoken API endpoint has the same vulnerability as the endpoint used by Solarman. However, the JWT token that is generated contains a wrong value and is not accepted by the server, even though the token is valid and signed. This issue could pose a future risk if not properly patched​.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Fol\u003E\n        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-card kg-file-card kg-file-card-medium\"\u003E\n            \u003Ca class=\"kg-file-card-container\" href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Ffiles\u002F2024\u002F08\u002FBitdefender-PReport-deye-creat7907.pdf\" title=\"Download\" download\u003E\n                \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-contents\"\u003E\n                    \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-title\"\u003EVulnerabilities in the Deye platform\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                    \n                    \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-metadata\"\u003E\n                        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-filename\"\u003EBitdefender-PReport-deye-creat7907.pdf\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                        \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-filesize\"\u003E2 MB\u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                    \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n                \u003Cdiv class=\"kg-file-card-icon\"\u003E\n                    \u003Csvg xmlns=\"http:\u002F\u002Fwww.w3.org\u002F2000\u002Fsvg\" viewBox=\"0 0 24 24\"\u003E\u003Cdefs\u003E\u003Cstyle\u003E.a{fill:none;stroke:currentColor;stroke-linecap:round;stroke-linejoin:round;stroke-width:1.5px;}\u003C\u002Fstyle\u003E\u003C\u002Fdefs\u003E\u003Ctitle\u003Edownload-circle\u003C\u002Ftitle\u003E\u003Cpolyline class=\"a\" points=\"8.25 14.25 12 18 15.75 14.25\"\u002F\u003E\u003Cline class=\"a\" x1=\"12\" y1=\"6.75\" x2=\"12\" y2=\"18\"\u002F\u003E\u003Ccircle class=\"a\" cx=\"12\" cy=\"12\" r=\"11.25\"\u002F\u003E\u003C\u002Fsvg\u003E\n                \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n            \u003C\u002Fa\u003E\n        \u003C\u002Fdiv\u003E\n        \u003Ch2 id=\"implications-for-grid-security\"\u003EImplications for Grid Security\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhile most of our previous findings have a serious impact on the individual or on the internet itself, the flaws detailed in this research are fundamentally different. Access to devices interacting with the grid can have devastating effects on the proper functioning of the grid itself. These vulnerabilities pose a significant threat to grid security in several ways:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EUnauthorized Control\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Attackers can take over accounts and control solar inverters, disrupting power generation and potentially causing voltage fluctuations.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EData Breaches\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: Sensitive information about users and organizations can be leaked, leading to privacy violations, information harvesting, targeted phishing attacks or other malicious activities.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EOperational Disruptions\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E: By accessing and modifying settings on solar inverters, attackers can cause widespread disruptions in power distribution, impacting grid stability and potentially leading to blackouts.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"disclosure-timeline\"\u003EDisclosure Timeline\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"solarman\"\u003ESolarman:\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 22, 2024: Bitdefender reaches out to Solarman for a security contact\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 23, 2024: Bitdefender gets in touch with Solarman security team and sends vulnerability information\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 24, 2024: Vulnerabilities acknowledged; account takeover gets immediately fixed\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJun 17, 2024: Vendor confirms that a fix for the API returning too much information is in place.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJul 17, 2024: Vendor confirms that the Deye token reuse issue is now fixed.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"deye\"\u003EDeye:\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\u003Cli\u003EMay 22, 2024: Bitdefender reaches out to Deye for a security contact\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJun 03, 2024: Authorized security partner asks Bitdefender for details\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJun 06, 2024: Bitdefender sends the vulnerability report\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJun 17, 2024: Bitdefender asks for an update on the reported issues\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EJul 09, 2024: Deye sends an overview of the fixed issues\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003Cli\u003EAug 07, 2024: This report becomes public as per the coordinated vulnerability disclosure protocols.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"conclusion\"\u003EConclusion\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIntegrating solar power into the grid offers immense benefits, but it also introduces attack surfaces that equipment makers must take into account. The security flaws found in the Deye and Solarman platforms highlight the need for robust cybersecurity in managing solar energy systems, as well as in general IoT setups.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EProtecting the grid from cyber threats is crucial to ensuring reliable and secure power for all. As we continue to embrace renewable energy, we must also remain vigilant and proactive in securing our energy infrastructure against evolving threats.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe’d like to thank the vendors involved for acknowledging and fixing these vulnerabilities in a timely manner.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EToo long for a read? Watch this piece of research as a video presentation on August 9 at 5\u003C\u002Fem\u003E:00PM (Creator Stage 2\u003Cem\u003E @ the IoT Village talks) at Defcon. If you are attending Defcon 32 in person, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.iotvillage.org\u002Fdefcon.html\"\u003Ecome see the talk live\u003C\u002Fa\u003E or meet and greet the researchers.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"appendix\"\u003EAppendix\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHere is a list of Solarman partner companies using the platform and potentially affected by the reported vulnerabilities. Our testing reveals that most of these vendors have scheduled maintentance windows to deploy security patches.
